<!-- define-1: body_md -->
\label{DEF:cr}
	Let $C$ be a field, $U$ be a $C$-linear space and $\rho$ be a $C$-linear operator on $U$.
	\begin{itemize}
		\item[(i)] Another  $C$-linear operator $\phi$ on $U$ is called a {\em complete reduction for $(U,\, \rho)$} if $U = \operatorname{im}(\rho) \oplus \operatorname{im}(\phi)$ and $\phi$ is the projection from $U$ to $\operatorname{im}(\phi)$ with respect to the direct sum\footnote{The first condition $U=\operatorname{im}(\rho) \oplus \operatorname{im}(\phi)$ means that every element $u$ in $U$ can be uniquely written as $q+w$ for $q\in \operatorname{im}(\rho)$ and $w\in \operatorname{im}(\phi)$; the second condition that $\phi$ is the projection from $U$ to $\operatorname{im}(\phi)$ with respect to the direct sum means that $\phi$ itself establishes this direct sum representation by mapping $u$ to the component $w$.}.
		
		\item[(ii)] Let $\phi$ be a complete reduction for $(U,\rho)$ and $u \in U$. If there is  $v \in U$ such that $\rho(v)=u-\phi(u)$, then $(v, \phi(u))$ is called a {\em $\Sigma$-pair with respect to $\phi$}.

		\item[(iii)] 	We say that {\em there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction
			for $(U, \rho)$} if we can fix a subspace $W$ such that $U=\operatorname{im}(\rho)\oplus W$, and there is an algorithm that, for every $u \in U$, computes 
		$v \in U$ and $w \in W$ such that $u = \rho(v) +w$. 
Note that such an algorithm induces a complete reduction for $(U, \rho)$ by mapping $u$ to $w$.
	\end{itemize}
<!-- end define-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{RE:cr}
	An easy linear algebra argument reveals that a $C$-linear operator $\phi$ is a complete reduction for $(U, \rho)$ 
	if and only if it is an idempotent whose kernel is equal to $\operatorname{im}(\rho)$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{RE:sigma+notation}
$a\sigma(a^{\sigma,i})=a^{\sigma,i+1}$ and $a^i\sigma(a^{\sigma,\sigma,i})=a^{\sigma,\sigma,i+1}$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- define-2: body_md -->
\label{DEF:computable}
A ring/field ${\mathbb{A}}$ is called {\em computable} if all ring/field operations are computable and the zero-recognition problem is solvable. A field ${\mathbb{F}}$ is said to be {\em factorizable} if it is computable and one can additionally factorize multivariate polynomials over ${\mathbb{F}}$. Moreover, a field ${\mathbb{F}}$ is said to be {\em orbit-computable} if it is factorizable, and for any $c_1,\dots,c_n \in {\mathbb{F}}$, one can compute  a ${\mathbb{Z}}$-basis of
$$M(c_1,\dots,c_n; {\mathbb{F}})= \{(e_1,\dots,e_n) \in \mathbb{Z}^n\mid 1=\prod_{i=1}^n c_i^{e_i}\}.$$
 A difference ring/field $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is said to be \emph{computable} if both ${\mathbb{A}}$ and the function $\sigma:{\mathbb{A}}\to{\mathbb{A}}$ are computable.
<!-- end define-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{Remark:AlgRatOribt}
Any  field of multivariate rational functions over an algebraic number field is orbit-computable~\cite{Ge:93a,Schneider:2005}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- convention-1: body_md -->
Throughout the paper,
\begin{itemize}
\item [(i)] all rings are commutative with 1 and have characteristic $0$,
 \item[(ii)] whenever $U$ as given in Definition \ref{DEF:cr} is a difference ring with constant 
 field $C$,  $U$ is regarded as a $C$-linear space and every operator on $U$ is understood to be $C$-linear, unless otherwise specified.
 \end{itemize}
<!-- end convention-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{LM:RPiSigma+term}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring and $\ell \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$.
\begin{itemize}
  \item [(i)] Assume that $t$ is an $S$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ with $\sigma(t)=t+a$ for some $a \in {\mathbb{A}} \backslash \{0\}$. Then $\sigma^{\ell}(t)=t+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\sigma^i(a)$.
  \item [(ii)] Assume that $t$ is an $AP$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ with $\sigma(t)=bt$ for some $b \in {\mathbb{A}}^*$. Then $\sigma^{\ell}(t)=b^{\sigma, \ell}t$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- define-3: body_md -->
A difference field $({\mathbb{F}},\,\sigma)$ with constant field $C$ is called {\em a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-field } over $C$ if $({\mathbb{F}},\sigma)$ is built by a tower of difference field extensions ${\mathbb{F}}=C(t_1)\dots(t_e)$ with the same constant field
$C$, where, for every $i\in [e]$, we have 
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item [(i)] $\sigma(t_i)/t_i\in C(t_1)\dots(t_{i-1})^*$~($t_i$ is called a {\em $\Pi$-field monomial}), or
		\item [(ii)] $\sigma(t_i)-t_i\in C(t_1)\dots(t_{i-1})$~($t_i$ is called a {\em $\Sigma^*$-field monomial}).
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end define-3 -->
<!-- define-4: body_md -->
\label{DEF:RPiSigma}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be a difference ring and $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ be a tower of difference ring extensions of $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$,
\begin{equation} \label{EQ:tower}
	\begin{array}{cccccccc}
		{\mathbb{A}}={\mathbb{A}}_0       & \leq &  {\mathbb{A}}_1      & \leq & \cdots         & \leq     & {\mathbb{A}}_n={\mathbb{E}}  \\
		&         & \shortparallel &         &            &             & \shortparallel \\
		&         &  {\mathbb{A}}_0\langle t_1 \rangle &         &                   &  & {\mathbb{A}}_{n-1}\langle t_n \rangle.
	\end{array}
\end{equation}
If $t_i$ is an $A$-/$P$-/$S$-/$AP$-/$PS$-/$APS$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}_{i-1}$ for every $i\in [n]$, then $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ is called an {\em $A$-/$P$-/$S$-/$AP$-/$PS$-/$APS$-tower} over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$.
\noindent A difference ring extension $({\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle, \, \sigma)$ is called an {\em  $R$-/$\Pi$-/$\Sigma^*$-/$R\Pi$-/$\Pi\Sigma^*$-/$R\Pi\Sigma^*$- extension} of $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ if $t$ is an $A$-/$P$-/$S$-/$AP$-/$PS$-/$APS$-monomial and $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle, \sigma)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. Moreover, the difference ring extension ${\mathbb{E}}$ as given in \eqref{EQ:tower} is called an {\em  $R$-/$\Pi$-/$\Sigma^*$-/$R\Pi$-/$\Pi\Sigma^*$-/$R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower} over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$~(or {\em $R$-/$\Pi$-/$\Sigma^*$-/$R\Pi$-/$\Pi\Sigma^*$-/$R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower} over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$) if $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$.
<!-- end define-4 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{THM:testingRPiSigma-monomials}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring, where the set of constants forms a field. Then the following assertions hold.
\begin{itemize}
  \item [(i)] Let $({\mathbb{A}}[t],\, \sigma)$ be an $S$-extension of $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ with $\sigma(t)=t+a$ for $a\in {\mathbb{A}}$. Then it is an $\Sigma^*$-extension if and only if there is
  no $g\in {\mathbb{A}}$ such that $\sigma(g)=g+a$.
  \item [(ii)] Let $({\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}],\, \sigma)$ be a $P$-extension of $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ with $\sigma(t)=b\,t$ for $b\in {\mathbb{A}}^*$. Then it is an $\Pi$-extension if and only if there are no $g\in {\mathbb{A}}\backslash \{0\}$ and $m \in {\mathbb{Z}}\backslash \{0\}$ such that $\sigma(g)=b^mg$.
  \item [(iii)] Let $({\mathbb{A}}[t], \,\sigma)$ be an $A$-extension of $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ of order $\lambda>1$ with $\sigma(t)=\alpha t$ for $\alpha \in {\mathbb{A}}^*$. Then it is an $R$-extension if and only if there are no $g\in {\mathbb{A}}\backslash \{0\}$ and $m \in [\lambda-1]$ such that $\sigma(g)=\alpha^mg$. If it is an $R$-extension, then $\alpha$ is primitive, i.e., $\operatorname{ord}(\alpha)=\lambda$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
Let ${\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{Q}}(x)$ be the rational difference field with  $\sigma(x)=x+1$. 
 \begin{itemize}
   \item [(i)] Take the unimonomial ring extension $({\mathbb{F}}[t],\sigma)$ of $({\mathbb{F}},\sigma)$ with $\sigma(t)=t+\frac{1}{x+1}$. Then $t$, which models the harmonic numbers $H_n:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{i}$, is a $\Sigma^*$-monomial over ${\mathbb{F}}$. 
   \item [(ii)] Let $({\mathbb{F}}[t, t^{-1}],\sigma)$ be a difference ring extension of ${\mathbb{F}}$ with $\sigma(t)=(x+1)t$. Then $t$, which models $n!$, is a $\Pi$-monomial over ${\mathbb{F}}$. 
   \item [(iii)] Take the algebraic extension $({\mathbb{F}}[t], \sigma)$ with $\sigma(t)=-t$ of order 2. Then it is an $R$-extension subject to the relation $t^2=1$. And $t$ represents  $(-1)^n$.
 \end{itemize}
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{PROP:orderRPiSigma}
Let $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ be an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over a difference ring $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ which is an integral domain. Then it can be reordered to the form 
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:reoder+RPiSigma}
{\mathbb{A}}\langle y_1 \rangle \ldots \langle y_{\ell} \rangle \langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_{m} \rangle  \langle s_1 \rangle \ldots \langle s_{n} \rangle,
\end{equation}
where the $y_i$ are $R$-monomials, the $t_i$ are $\Pi$-monomials and the $s_i$ are $\Sigma^*$-monomials.
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The proof for the case where ${\mathbb{A}}$ is a field is given in~\cite[Lemma~4.13]{Schn2016}. Since this argument does not rely on the invertibility of elements, it carries over straightforwardly to the setting where ${\mathbb{A}}$ is an integral domain by replacing \cite[Corollary~4.3]{Schn2016} with the following claim.

\noindent{\em Claim.} Let $({\mathbb{A}}\langle y_1\rangle \ldots \langle y_\ell \rangle,\sigma)$ be an $R$-tower over a difference ring $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ which is integral. Then it contains no nonzero nilpotent elements.

\noindent{\em Proof of the claim.}  Set $\lambda_i=\operatorname{ord}(y_i)$ for $i\in[\ell]$. Let $H$ be the polynomial ring ${\mathbb{A}}[ y_1] \ldots [y_\ell]$ and $I\subset H$ be the ideal generated by $y_1^{\lambda_1}-1,\ldots,y_\ell^{\lambda_\ell}-1$. Then ${\mathbb{A}}\langle y_1\rangle \ldots \langle y_\ell \rangle$ is isomorphic to the quotient ring $H/I$. It remains to show that $I$ is radical. 
Assume that $h\in H$ satisfies $h^n\in I$.
Let $Q({\mathbb{A}})$ denote the quotient field of ${\mathbb{A}}$ and let $I_Q\subset Q({\mathbb{A}})[ y_1] \ldots [y_\ell]$ be the ideal generated by  $y_1^{\lambda_1}-1,\ldots,y_\ell^{\lambda_\ell}-1$. It is radical by \cite[Lemma~4.2]{Schn2016}. Note that $h^n\in I \subset I_Q$. So $h\in I_Q$.  Hence, there are $g_1,\ldots,g_r\in Q({\mathbb{A}})[ y_1] \ldots [y_\ell]$ such that $h=g_1 (y_1^{\lambda_1}-1)+\cdots +g_\ell (y_\ell^{\lambda_\ell}-1)$. Since the generators of $I_Q$ form a Gröbner basis of $I_Q$ with respect to the lexicographic order and are monic in $H$, we get $g_1,\ldots,g_\ell\in H$, which implies that $h\in I$. Thus, $I$ is radical.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- define-5: body_md -->
\label{DEF:stable}
  A difference ring $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ is said to be {\em constant-stable} if $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^k)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ for every $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$.
<!-- end define-5 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{RE:constant-stable}
If the difference ring $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ is constant-stable, then  $({\mathbb{A}}, \sigma^{\lambda})$ is constant-stable for every $\lambda \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{Lemma:ConstantStable}
Let $({\mathbb{E}}, \,\sigma)$ be a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over a difference ring $({\mathbb{A}}, \,\sigma)$. If $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ is a constant-stable integral domain, then so is $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We prove the lemma by induction on the number of $\Pi\Sigma^*$-monomials. If ${\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{A}}$, the statement clearly holds. Otherwise, suppose that the statement has been proven for a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ and let $t$ be  $\Pi\Sigma^*$-monomial over ${\mathbb{E}}$.	
	Clearly, ${\mathbb{E}}\langle t\rangle$ is an integral domain. Hence
	it suffices to show that $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}}\langle t\rangle, \sigma^k)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ for all $k>1$. The conclusion that $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma) \subset \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}}\langle t\rangle, \sigma^k)$ is immediate. Conversely, let $f \in {\mathbb{E}} \langle t \rangle$ satisfy $\sigma^k(f)=f$ for some $k>1$. Suppose that $f \notin {\mathbb{E}}$. Define $h:=f^{\sigma,k}=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1} \sigma^i(f)$. 
	Since $\deg_t(\sigma^i(f))=\deg_t(f)$ for every $i \in {\mathbb{N}}$ by Lemma \ref{LM:RPiSigma+term}, it follows $h \notin{\mathbb{E}}$. On the other hand,
	$f\sigma(h)=f^{\sigma, k+1}=h\sigma^{k}(f)=hf.$
	Since ${\mathbb{E}}$ is an integral domain, we have $\sigma(h)=h$. Together with $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}}\langle t \rangle, \sigma)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$, $h \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$. So $h \in {\mathbb{E}}$, a contradiction. Thus, $f \in {\mathbb{E}}$. Furthermore, $f \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ because ${\mathbb{E}}$ is constant-stable. Consequently,
	$\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}}\langle t\rangle, \sigma^k)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$, which completes the induction step.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{PROP:R-monomial+primitive}
 Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring and $t$ be an $A$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ of order $\lambda$. Assume that ${\mathbb{A}}$ is a constant-stable integral domain. Then $\alpha:=\sigma(t)/t \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. In particular, $t$ is an $R$-monomial if and only if $\alpha$ is a $\lambda$th primitive root of unity in $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$, i.e.,\ $\operatorname{ord}(\alpha)=\lambda$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Since $\alpha$ is a root of unity in ${\mathbb{A}}$, there is $n \in {\mathbb{N}}$ such that $\alpha^n=1$. For every $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}$, applying $\sigma^i$ to the above equation yields $(\sigma^i(\alpha))^n=1$. Let $x$ be an indeterminate over ${\mathbb{A}}$. Then $\alpha$, $\sigma(\alpha)$, $\ldots$, $\sigma^i(\alpha)$, $\ldots$ are roots of $x^n-1$. Note that ${\mathbb{A}}$ is an integral domain. So $x^n-1$ has at most $n$ roots in ${\mathbb{A}}$. Hence, there are $i,j \in {\mathbb{Z}}$ with $i >j$ such that $\sigma^i(\alpha)=\sigma^j(\alpha)$, which implies  $\alpha \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{i-j})$. Furthermore, $\alpha \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma$) by $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \sigma^k)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ for $k\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$. Note that \cite[Proposition~2.20]{Schn2017} admits a mild generalization from fields to integral domains. So $t$ is an $R$-monomial if and only if $\alpha$ is a $\lambda$th primitive root of unity in $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- define-6: body_md -->
\label{DEF:SimpleRPiSigma}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be a difference ring with constant field $C$ and ${G}$ be a multiplicative subgroup of ${\mathbb{A}}^*$.  Let $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ be an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1 \rangle \ldots \langle t_n\rangle$, and define the multiplicative group
$${{G}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}}:=\{ft_1^{k_1}\ldots t_n^{k_n}~|~f\in {G} \text{ and }k_i\in {\mathbb{Z}}~\text{with}~k_i=0~\text{if $t_i$ is a $\Sigma^*$-monomial}\}.$$
We say that ${\mathbb{E}}$ is {\em ${G}$-simple} if for any $R\Pi$-monomial $t_i$, we have $\sigma(t_i)/t_i \in {{G}}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}}$.
Any such extension is said to be {\em simple} if it is ${\mathbb{A}}^*$-simple.
<!-- end define-6 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{RE:SimplePiSigma=PiSigma}
 A $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over a difference integral domain $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ is simple by \cite[Proposition~4.14]{Schn2016}. Thus, it is precisely the presence of $R$-monomials, or more generally the situation where ${\mathbb{A}}$ is not a field (or not an integral domain), that gives rise to broader classes of $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-towers which are not simple,
  for a concrete example, we refer to Example~\ref{EX:CheckRmonomials} below. Complete reductions for this general case will be treated in Section~\ref{SECT:IdemotentRepresentation} below.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{EX:assumptionPi}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}],\,\sigma)$ be a $\Pi$-extension of a difference ring $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ with $\sigma(t)=ft$ for some $f\in {\mathbb{A}}^*$. We aim to determine whether $ut$ with $u\in {\mathbb{A}}$ is summable in ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$. 
Let $q=\sum_nq_nt^n\in {\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$ be such that $ut=\Delta(q)$. A direct computation shows that $\Delta(q_nt^n)=(f^n\sigma(q_n)-q_n)t^n$
for every $n\in {\mathbb{Z}}$. In particular, $f^n\sigma(q_n)-q_n=0$, or equivalently $\sigma(q_n)=f^{-n}q_n$ for all $n\neq1$. Since $t$ is a $\Pi$-monomial, {we have $q_n=0$ by Theorem~\ref{THM:testingRPiSigma-monomials}~(ii)}.  Thus, if there is a solution $ut=\Delta(q)$ with $q\in{\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$, then $q=yt$ for some $y\in{\mathbb{A}}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{Equ:FirstOrderEq}
u=f\sigma(y)-y.
\end{equation} 
Conversely, if $y\in{\mathbb{A}}$ is a solution of~\eqref{Equ:FirstOrderEq},
then $\Delta(y\,t)=v\,t$. Note that~\eqref{Equ:FirstOrderEq} is a typical first-order difference equation that also appears in Karr's algorithm~\cite{Karr1981} or is related to the so-called Gosper equation~\cite{Gosp1978}.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
Let ${\mathbb{A}}[t]$ be an $R$-extension of a difference ring $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ of order $\lambda$ with $\sigma(t)=at$. For $f=st\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]$ and $g\in {\mathbb{A}}$, we determine whether there is a $q\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]$ such that $g=f\sigma(q)-q$. If such a $q$ exists, write it as $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}q_it^i$ with $q_i \in {\mathbb{A}}$. Then 
$$g=(sa^{\lambda-1}\sigma(q_{\lambda-1})-q_0)+
\sum_{j=1}^{\lambda-1}(s\sigma(q_{\lambda-j-1})a^{\lambda-j-1}-q_{\lambda-j})t^{\lambda-j}.$$
Since $g \in {\mathbb{A}}$, we have  
$q_{\lambda-j}=s\sigma(q_{\lambda-j-1})a^{\lambda-j-1}$ for every $j \in [\lambda-1]$. By iterative substitution, it follows that 
$g=s^{\sigma, \lambda}a^{\sigma,\sigma, \lambda}\sigma^{\lambda}(q_0)-q_0.$
So $g \in \operatorname{im}(\Delta_f)$ if and only if $p\sigma^{\lambda}(y)-y=g$ with $p=s^{\sigma, \lambda}a^{\sigma,\sigma, \lambda}$ has a solution in ${\mathbb{A}}$.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- define-7: body_md -->
\label{DEF:Generalizedkarrequation}
    Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring with constant field $C$. For $f \in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, define
    \begin{equation} \label{EQ:Generalizedkarrequation}
 \begin{array}{cccc}
\Delta_f^{(i)}: & {\mathbb{A}} & \longrightarrow & {\mathbb{A}}  \\
      &  y   & \mapsto         & f\sigma^i(y)-y. 
\end{array} 
\end{equation}
<!-- end define-7 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\cite{Karr1981, Schn2016}\label{Thm:PiSiOverSimpleRPiSiDESolver}
Let $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ be a simple $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-field $({\mathbb{F}},\,\sigma)$ with orbit-computable\footnote{If the $\Pi\Sigma^*$-field $({\mathbb{F}},\sigma)$ over $C$ is built only by $\Sigma^*$-field monomials, it suffices to require that $C$ is factorizable.} constant field $C$. Then, for $f\in {({\mathbb{F}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{F}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}}$, $i\in{\mathbb{Z}}^+$ and $g\in{\mathbb{E}}$, one can decide algorithmically if there is $q\in{\mathbb{E}}$  such that $\Delta^{(i)}_f(q)=g$.
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{LM:diffiso+CR}
 Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ and $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \tilde{\sigma})$ be difference rings. Assume that the set of constants of ${\mathbb{A}}$ and that of $\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}$ are fields, and there is a difference ring isomorphism $\tau$ from $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \tilde{\sigma})$ onto $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. Let $h \in\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}^*$.  Assume further that $\phi_{\tau(h)}$ is a complete reduction  for $({\mathbb{A}}, \,\Delta_{\tau(h)})$. Then the following holds.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] For $\tilde{\Delta}_h:=h\tilde{\sigma}-\bf{1}$, we have $\tilde{\Delta}_h=\tau^{-1} \circ \Delta_{\tau(h)} \circ \tau$.
  \item [(ii)]~$\psi_h:=\tau^{-1} \circ \phi_{\tau(h)} \circ \tau$ is a complete reduction for $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \tilde{\Delta}_h)$, and for every $g\in \tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}$, $(\tau^{-1}(u), \tau^{-1}(v))$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_h$ if  $(u, v)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $\tau(g)$ with respect to $\phi_{\tau(h)}$.
  \end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
~(i)  For all $s \in \tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}$, $(\tau^{-1} \circ \Delta_{\tau(h)} \circ \tau)(s)=h \tilde{\sigma}(s)-s$ by $\sigma\circ\tau=\tau\circ\tilde{\sigma}$. So $\tilde{\Delta}_h=\tau^{-1} \circ \Delta_{\tau(h)} \circ \tau$.

(ii) First we show that $\psi_h$ is a complete reduction for $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \tilde{\Delta}_h)$.
Since $\phi_{\tau(h)}$ is idempotent, so is $\psi_h$. 
By Remark \ref{RE:cr}, it suffices  to verify that $\ker(\psi_h)=\operatorname{im}(\tilde{\Delta}_h)$.  
Since $\psi_h = \tau^{-1} \circ \phi_{\tau(h)} \circ \tau$, we have $\ker(\psi_h) = \tau^{-1}(\ker(\phi_{\tau(h)})).$
By (i), 
$\operatorname{im}(\tilde{\Delta}_h) =\tau^{-1} \operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\tau(h)}) $.
Thus, $\ker(\psi_h)=\operatorname{im}(\tilde{\Delta}_h)$
by $\ker(\phi_{\tau(h)})=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\tau(h)})$.

 Note that $\tau(g)=\Delta_{\tau(h)}(u)+v$. Applying $\tau^{-1}$ to the above equation, we obtain $g=\tilde{\Delta}_h (\tau^{-1}(u))+\tau^{-1}(v)$ by (i).
Moreover, $\tau^{-1}(v) \in \operatorname{im}(\psi_h)$ by $v \in \operatorname{im}(\phi_{\tau(h)})$. So  $(\tau^{-1}(u), \tau^{-1}(v))$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_h$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{LM:GeneralRational}
 Let $(C(x),\,\sigma)$ be the rational difference field with $\sigma(x)=x+1$. Assume that there is an algorithm which,  for every $f\in C(x)^*$,  constructs a complete reduction $\phi_f$ for $(C(x), \Delta_f)$. Then there is an algorithm that, for every $f \in C(x)^*$ and $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, constructs a complete reduction $\phi_f^{(i)}$ for $(C(x), \Delta_f^{(i)})$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $f\in C(x)^*$. Define
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
\tau: & (C(x), \sigma^i) & \rightarrow & (C(x), \sigma) \\
      & w(x) & \mapsto     & w(ix).
\end{array}
\]
Since $\sigma\circ\tau=\tau\circ\sigma^i$, $(C(x),\, \sigma)$ and $(C(x), \sigma^{i})$ are isomorphic. By the assumption, there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction for $(C(x),\, \Delta_{\tau(f)})$. This yields an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction for $(C(x),\, \Delta_{f}^{(i)})$ by replacing $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\,\tilde{\sigma})$ and  $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ with $(C(x),\, \sigma^i)$ and $(C(x),\, \sigma)$ in Lemma \ref{LM:diffiso+CR}, respectively.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{LM:ks}
Let $f\in C(x)$ with a normal form $(\xi, \eta)$. Then the following assertions hold.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $\Delta_f = \eta^{-1} \circ \Delta_\xi \circ \eta$, 
where $\eta$ is understood as a $C$-linear operator on $C(x)$  with $g \mapsto \eta g$ for all $g \in C(x)$.
\item[(ii)] If $\phi_\xi$ is a complete reduction for $(C(x), \Delta_\xi)$, then $\phi_f:=\eta^{-1} \circ \phi_\xi  \circ \eta$
is a complete reduction for $(C(x), \Delta_f)$. Moreover, let $g \in C(x)$ and $(u, r)$ be a $\Sigma$-pair of $\eta g$ with respect to $\phi_{\xi}$, then
$(\eta^{-1}u, \eta^{-1}r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\phi_f$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
(i) For every $g\in C(x)$, we have $(\eta^{-1} \circ \Delta_\xi \circ \eta)(g) = (\xi\sigma(\eta)/\eta)\sigma(g) - g$
by a straightforward calculation. So $(\eta^{-1} \circ \Delta_\xi \circ \eta)(g)=\Delta_f(g)$ due to $f = \xi\sigma(\eta)/\eta$.
  
(ii) Since $\phi^2_{\xi}=\phi_{\xi}$, we have $\phi^2_f=\phi_f$.  To show $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) = \ker(\phi_f)$,
we note that $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) = \eta^{-1} \operatorname{im}(\Delta_\xi)$ by (i).
Furthermore, $\ker(\phi_f) = \eta^{-1} \ker(\phi_\xi)$  by the definitions of $\phi_\xi$ and $\phi_f$. Hence, $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) = \ker(\phi_f)$ because $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_\xi)=\ker(\phi_\xi)$.  Consequently, $\phi_f$ is a complete reduction for $(C(x), \Delta_f)$. 

Let $g\in C(x)$. Assume that $(u, r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $\eta g$ with respect to $\phi_{\xi}$. Then $\eta g=\Delta_{\xi}(u)+r$. Dividing both sides of this equation by $\eta$ and using (i), we obtain
$$g=\eta^{-1}\Delta_{\xi}(u)+\eta^{-1}r=(\eta^{-1}\circ\Delta_\xi \circ \eta\circ\eta^{-1})(u)+\eta^{-1}r=\Delta_f(\eta^{-1}u)+\eta^{-1}r.$$ 
Moreover, $\eta^{-1}r \in \operatorname{im}(\phi_f)$ by $\eta^{-1}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{\xi})=\operatorname{im}(\phi_f)$. Thus, $(\eta^{-1}u, \eta^{-1}r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\phi_f$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{PROP:CR+Rational}
Let $\xi\in C(x)$ be $\sigma$-reduced and the elements of $S$ are strongly $\sigma$-coprime with $\xi$. Then
$$C(x)=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\xi}) \oplus U_{S,\xi} \oplus \frac{V_{\xi}}{\operatorname{den}(\xi)},$$
where $U_{S,\xi}, V_{\xi}$ are given by \eqref{EQ:rationalcomplement} and \eqref{EQ:polynomialComplement}, respectively. Furthermore, the projection from $C(x)$ to $U_{S,\xi} \oplus \frac{V_{\xi}}{\operatorname{den}(\xi)}$ is a complete reduction $\phi_{\xi}$ for $(C(x), \Delta_{\xi})$. If $C$ is factorizable, $\Sigma$-pairs with respect to $\phi_{\xi}$ can be computed explicitly.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
First we prove $C(x)=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\xi}) + U_{S,\xi} + \frac{V_{\xi}}{\operatorname{den}(\xi)}$. Let $g\in C(x)$. 
Write it as $\tilde{g}+h$, where $\tilde{g} \in C[x]$ and $h \in C(x)$ with $\deg_x(\operatorname{num}(h))<\deg_x(\operatorname{den}(h))$.
 Let \eqref{Equ:SigmaFacFull} be a strong $\sigma$-factorization of $\operatorname{den}(g)$ with respect to $\xi$. Then by \cite[Lemma~3]{AP2001} or Algorithm {\tt ShellReduction} in \cite[\S 3]{CHKL2015}, one can compute $k_1,\ldots, k_s \in {\mathbb{N}}$, $a, h\in C(x)$ with $\deg_x(h)<\deg_x(\prod_{i=1}^{s}p_i^{k_i})$, and $v\in C[x]$ such that
 $g=\Delta_{\xi}(a)+h/\prod_{i=1}^{s}p_i^{k_i}+v/\operatorname{den}(\xi)$; note that here one requires a strong $\sigma$-factorization, which one can compute if $C$ is factorizable. 
 Set $u=h/\prod_{i=1}^{s}p_i^{k_i}$. Then $u \in U_{S,\xi}$. 
 Applying \cite[Lemma 4.2]{CHKL2015} to $v$, we obtain $b\in C[x]$ and $\tilde{v} \in V_{\xi}$ such that $v={\mathcal{P}}_{\xi}(b)+\tilde{v}$. 
 It follows that $g=\Delta_{\xi}(a+b)+u+\tilde{v}/\operatorname{den}(\xi)$.  On the other hand, $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\xi}) \cap (U_{S,\xi}+V_{\xi}/\operatorname{den}(\xi))=\{0\}$ by \cite[Proposition~3.5]{CDGHL2025}. Hence, $C(x)=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\xi}) \oplus (U_{S,\xi}+V_{\xi}/\operatorname{den}(\xi))$.\\
 Let $w \in U_{S,\xi}\cap V_{\xi}/\operatorname{den}(\xi)$. Suppose that $w \neq 0$. Then $\deg_x(\operatorname{den}(w))>0$. Since $\operatorname{den}(w)$ is strongly coprime with $\operatorname{den}(\xi)$, $\gcd(\operatorname{den}(w), \operatorname{den}(\xi))=1$. So $\operatorname{den}(\xi)w \notin C[x]$, which contradicts to $\operatorname{den}(\xi)w \in V_{\xi}$. Consequently, $C(x)=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_{\xi}) \oplus U_{S,\xi} \oplus V_{\xi}/\operatorname{den}(\xi)$. In particular, the map $\phi_{\xi}:C(x)\to  U_{S,\xi} \oplus V_{\xi}/\operatorname{den}(\xi)$ defined by $\phi_{\xi}(g)=u+\tilde{v}/\operatorname{den}(\xi)$ forms a complete reduction for $(C(x), \Delta_{\xi})$ and $(a+b,u+\tilde{v}/\operatorname{den}(\xi))$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to  $\phi_{\xi}$.  By construction, such a $\Sigma$-pair is constructed if $C$ is factorizable.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{PROP:Generalrational+highorder}
 Let $(C(x),\,\sigma)$ be the rational difference field with $\sigma(x)=x+1$, where $C$ is factorizable. Then, there is an algorithm that, for every $f\in C(x)^*$ and $i\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, constructs a complete reduction $\phi^{(i)}_f$ for $(C(x), \Delta^{(i)}_f)$.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
By Algorithm {\tt RNF} in \cite{AbPe2002b} (which is executable if $C$ is factorizable), we obtain a normal form $(\xi, \eta)$ of $f$. Note that $\xi$ is $\sigma$-reduced. So there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\phi_{\xi}$ for $(C(x), \Delta_{\xi})$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:CR+Rational}. In particular, let
  $g\in C(x)$. One can compute a $\Sigma$-pair  $(u, r)$ of $\eta g$ with respect to $\phi_{\xi}$. 
 It follows from Lemma \ref{LM:ks} that $\phi_f:= \eta^{-1} \circ \phi_{\xi} \circ \eta$ is a complete reduction for $(C(x), \Delta_f)$ and $(\eta^{-1}u, \eta^{-1}r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ under $\phi_f$.  Finally, we can apply Lemma \ref{LM:GeneralRational} to obtain the desired result.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- alg-1: body_md -->
{\tt CRForRationalFunctions}$(C(x), g, f,i)$ \label{ALG:CRForRationalFunctions}

 \noindent
{\tt Input:} $g, f \in C(x)$ with $f\neq 0$, $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$. 

\noindent From outside accessible: a table ${\mathbb{T}}$ indexed by every element $w$ in $C(x)$, the value of $w$ is $(\xi, \eta, S)$, where $(\xi, \eta)$ is a normal form of $w$ and $S\subseteq M_x$ whose elements are pairwise $\sigma$-coprime and strongly $\sigma$-coprime with $\xi$.

 \noindent
{\tt Output:} A $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\phi_f^{(i)}$ as given in Proposition~\ref{PROP:Generalrational+highorder}.

\smallskip \noindent
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[(1)] $(\tilde{g},\tilde{f}) \leftarrow g(ix), f(ix)$
\item[(2)] {\tt if} $\tilde{f}$ is an index of ${\mathbb{T}}$,  {\tt then}
\begin{itemize}
  \item [] $(\xi,\eta, S) \leftarrow$ the value of $\tilde{f}$ 
\end{itemize} 
 {\tt else}
 \begin{itemize}
   \item [] $(\xi, \eta) \leftarrow$  {\tt RNF}$(\tilde{f})$ in \cite{AbPe2002b}, $S \leftarrow \{\}$, add $\tilde{f}=(\xi, \eta, S)$ to ${\mathbb{T}}$
 \end{itemize}
 {\tt end if}
 
	\item[(3)] compute the $\Sigma$-pair $(u, r)$ of $\eta \tilde{g}$ with respect to\ $\phi_{\xi}$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:CR+Rational}.\\
During the computation, update $S$ to  $\tilde{S}$, and update the value of $\tilde{f}$ to  $(\xi, \eta, \tilde{S})$ in ${\mathbb{T}}$
	\item[(4)] {\tt return} $\left(\eta^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{i}\right)u\left(\frac{x}{i}\right), \eta^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{i}\right)r\left(\frac{x}{i}\right) \right)$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-1 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
The detailed procedure for updating $S$ to $\tilde{S}$ in step 3 as follows:
to find a $\Sigma$-pair of $\eta\tilde g$, we start with computing a strong $\sigma$-factorization of $\operatorname{den}(\eta \tilde{g})$ with respect to $\xi$.
 Let the factorization be given by \eqref{Equ:SigmaFacFull}. 
Then one check if there is $q\in S$ and $m\in {\mathbb{Z}}$ such that $\sigma^m(p_1)=q$. If this is the case, we replace $p_1$ with $q$, $k_1$, $\ell_1$ by $k_1-m$ and $\ell_1-m$, respectively. Otherwise, we add $p_1$ to $S$. Next we repeat this process for $p_2, \dots, p_s$, simultaneously completing the update of the set $S$.
Finally, we compute the desired $\Sigma$-pair by following the steps in the proof of Proposition~\ref{PROP:CR+Rational}.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
The reasons for taking ${\mathbb{T}}$ as a global variable in the above algorithm are as follows. When using the algorithm to compute $\Sigma$-pairs with respect to the same complete reduction $\phi_f$ for $(C(x),\Delta_f)$ with $f \in C(x)^*$, but for different inputs $g_1, g_2 \in C(x)$, we must guarantee that the same $\phi_f^{(i)}$ is used. This requires choosing the same $\xi$, $\eta$, and the same set $S$ whenever $f$ appears in multiple computations. Since $S$ is not fixed in advance but constructed online, we reuse previously generated elements (e.g., those for $g_1$) and update $S$ when new elements are needed (e.g., for $g_2$). This is achieved by introducing a global table ${\mathbb{T}}$, which stores, for every $w\in C(x)$, the triple $(\xi, \eta, S)$. In subsequent computations, we retrieve this information, thereby ensuring that the same complete reductions are always reused and, as a byproduct, avoiding redundant calculations.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
\label{EX:rational}
Let $f=-1$ and $g=1/(x+1)$. We are going to use Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForRationalFunctions} to compute a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\phi_f^{(2)}$. Initially, we set the table ${\mathbb{T}}$ to be empty. In step~$1$, $\tilde{f}=-1$ and $\tilde{g}=1/(2x+1)$. Then $\xi=-1$, $\eta=1$ and $S=\{\}$.
We add the entry $-1 \rightarrow (\xi, \eta, S)$ to ${\mathbb{T}}$ in step 2. Let $\phi_{\xi}$ be the complete reduction for $(C(x), \Delta_{\xi})$ as
given in Proposition~\ref{PROP:CR+Rational}. In addition, the proposition delivers  a $\Sigma$-pair $(0, \tilde{g})$ of $\eta \tilde{g}$ with respect to $\phi_{\xi}$ using $S=\{\}$. The entry corresponding to $-1$ in ${\mathbb{T}}$ is then updated to $(-1,1, [x+1/2])$. Finally, we get that $(0, 1/(x+1))$ is a  $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\phi_f^{(2)}$.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- convention-2: body_md -->
\label{CON:RCase}
In this section,  let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring with constant field $C$, and ${G}$ be a multiplicative subgroup of ${\mathbb{A}}^*$ that is closed under $\sigma$. Let $t$ be an $R$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ with $\alpha:=\sigma(t)/t \in {G}$ of order $\lambda$. Fix $f=st^m$, where $s\in G$ and $m\in [\lambda-1]_0$. For $\ell \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, set $a:=\alpha^{\sigma,\ell}$ and $\tilde \sigma:=\sigma^\ell$.
<!-- end convention-2 -->
<!-- hyp-1: body_md -->
\label{HYP:indR}
We assume  that there is a map that assigns to each element $z\in {G}$ and $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}^+$ a 
complete reduction $\phi_z^{(i)}$ for $({\mathbb{A}}, \Delta_z^{(i)})$ as a $C$-linear map.
<!-- end hyp-1 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{LM:FormularForKshift}
    Let $u_0:=u\in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $i\in [\lambda-1]_0$. Set 
    $ u_j= s\tilde\sigma(u_{j-1}) a^{i+(j-1)m}$ 
    for every $j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^+$. Then, for every $k \in {\mathbb{N}}$,
    \begin{itemize}
    \item [(i)]  $u_kt^{(i+km) \! \bmod \!\lambda}=u_0t^i+\Delta_f^{(\ell)}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}u_jt^{(i+jm) \! \bmod \!\lambda}\right);$
    \item [(ii)] $u_k=p_{i,k}\tilde\sigma^k(u_0)$ with $p_{i,k}=(sa^i)^{\tilde\sigma,k}(a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,k}$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Recall that $a:=\alpha^{\sigma,\ell}$. By Lemma~\ref{LM:RPiSigma+term} (ii), 
	\begin{equation}\label{EQ:Rimage}
		\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(ut^i)=s\tilde\sigma(u)a^it^{(m+i) \! \bmod \!\lambda}-ut^i.
	\end{equation}
	We proceed the lemma by induction on $k$.
		
	(i) The conclusion is evident for the case $k=0$.
	Assume that $k\ge 0$  and  the conclusion holds for $k$. Set $w:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}u_jt^{(i+jm) \! \bmod \!\lambda}$. By the induction hypothesis, \eqref{EQ:Rimage} and the fact that $a^{\lambda}=1$,  we have
	\begin{align*}
		\Delta_f^{(\ell)}\left(w\right)&=\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(u_kt^{(i+km) \! \bmod \!\lambda}) + \Delta_f^{(\ell)}\biggl(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}u_jt^{(i+jm)\! \bmod \!\lambda}\biggr) \\
		& = \Delta_f^{(\ell)}(u_kt^{(i+km) \! \bmod \!\lambda}) + u_kt^{(i+km) \! \bmod \!\lambda}-u_0t^i \\
		&=s\tilde{\sigma}(u_k)a^{i+km}t^{(i+(k+1)m)\! \bmod \!\lambda}-u_kt^{(i+km)\! \bmod \!\lambda}+ u_kt^{(i+km) \! \bmod \!\lambda}-u_0t^i\\
		&=u_{k+1}t^{(i+(k+1)m)\! \bmod \!\lambda}-u_0t^i.  
	\end{align*}

	(ii) The case $k=0$ holds trivially. Assume that $k>0$ and the conclusion holds for $k$. Then by the induction hypothesis and Remark \ref{RE:sigma+notation}, 
	\begin{align*}
		u_{k+1}&=s\tilde{\sigma}(u_k)a^{i+km}=s \tilde{\sigma}((sa^i)^{\tilde\sigma,k}(a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,k}\tilde{\sigma}^k(u_0))a^{i+km}\\
		&=sa^i\tilde{\sigma}((sa^i)^{\tilde\sigma,k})a^{km}\tilde{\sigma}((a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,k})\tilde\sigma^{k+1}(u_0)\\
		&=(sa^i)^{\tilde\sigma,k+1}(a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,k+1}\tilde\sigma^{k+1}(u_0)=p_{i,k+1}\tilde\sigma^{k+1}(u_0).
	\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{LM:ReductionForRCase}
	Let $u\in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $i\in [\lambda-1]_0$. Set $d:=\gcd(\lambda, m)$ and $\mu:=\lambda/d$. Then there are $q \in {\mathbb{A}} \langle t \rangle$, $ i'\in [d-1]_0$ and $r \in \operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i',\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})$ with $p_{i',\mu}=(sa^{i'})^{\tilde\sigma,\mu}(a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,\mu}$ such that
	$ut^i=\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q)+rt^{i'}$. If $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable and Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR} is constructive, such $q$ and $r$ can be computed.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let  $i= i' \bmod d$. Then using the extended Euclidean algorithm, there is $k\in {\mathbb{N}}$ such that $i'=(i+km) \bmod \lambda$.
For every $j\in {\mathbb{N}}$, set $p_{i,j}=(sa^i)^{\tilde\sigma,j}(a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,j}$. By Lemma~\ref{LM:FormularForKshift}, there is $q_1=-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1} p_{i,j}\tilde\sigma^j(u) t^{(i+jm) \! \bmod \!\lambda}\in {\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle$ and $r'= p_{i,k}\tilde\sigma^k(u)\in {\mathbb{A}}$ such that $ut^i=\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q_1)+r't^{i'}$.
Since $s,a\in{G}$ and ${G}$ is a multiplicative group that is closed under $\sigma$, we have $p_{i',\mu}\in {G}$. Further, by Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR}, there is $v\in {\mathbb{A}}$ such that $r'=\Delta^{(\mu\ell)}_{p_{i',\mu}}(v)+\phi_{p_{i',\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)}(r')$.
Using Lemma~\ref{LM:FormularForKshift} again, we get
$$\Delta^{(\mu\ell)}_{p_{i',\mu}}(v)t^{i'}=p_{i',\mu} \tilde\sigma^\mu(v)t^{(i'+\mu m)\! \bmod \!\lambda}-vt^{i'}=\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q_2)$$
with $q_2=\sum_{j=0}^{\mu-1} p_{i',j}\tilde\sigma^j(v) t^{(i'+jm) \! \bmod \!\lambda} \in {\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle$, 
which implies that $r't^{i'}=\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q_2)+\phi_{p_{i',\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)} (r')t^{i'}$.
So $ut^i =\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q_1+q_2)+\phi_{p_{i',\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)}(r')t^{i'}$. Therefore, the proof is completed by letting $q=q_1+q_2$ and $r=\phi_{p_{i',\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)}(r')$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{LM:ComplementforRExt}
Let $d:=\gcd(\lambda,m)$, $\mu:= \lambda/ d$, and $p_{i,\mu}:=(sa^i)^{\tilde\sigma,\mu}(a^m)^{\tilde\sigma,\tilde\sigma,\mu}$. Then
    $${\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle=\operatorname{im} (\Delta_f^{(\ell)})\oplus \biggl(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i,\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})t^i\biggr).$$ 
    In particular, the projection from ${\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$ to $ \sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i,\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})t^i$ is a complete reduction $\psi_f^{(\ell)}$ for $({\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle,\, \Delta_f^{(\ell)})$. 
    Moreover, if $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable and Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR} is constructive, one can compute a $\Sigma$-pair of any element of ${\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$  with respect to $\psi_f^{(\ell)}$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Recall that $C=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle,\sigma)$ because $t$ is an $R$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$. Then the $\operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i,\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})t^i$ are $C$-subspaces of ${\mathbb{A}} \langle t \rangle$, which implies that	
$W:=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i,\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})t^i$ forms a $C$-subspace of ${\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle $. 
 Every element in $ {\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle$ can be uniquely written as $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}g_it^i$, where $g_i \in {\mathbb{A}}$. 
 By Lemma~\ref{LM:ReductionForRCase}, we obtain $g_it^i=\Delta^{(\ell)}_f(q_i)+r_{i}$, where
 $q_i\in{\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$ and $r_{i}\in \operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i',\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})t^{i'}$ for some $i'\in [d-1]_0$. Consequently, by setting $q=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}q_i\in{\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$ and $r=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}r_{i}\in W$, we conclude that $g=\Delta^{(\ell)}_f(q)+r$. 
 Hence, ${\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle=\operatorname{im} (\Delta_f^{(\ell)})+W$. If $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable and Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR} is constructive, such $q$ and $r$ can be computed explicitly.
 
    Assume that $g\in \operatorname{im} (\Delta_f^{(\ell)})\cap W$. Then $g=\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q)$ for some $q\in {\mathbb{A}}\langle t \rangle$. Write $q$ as $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}q_i t^i$, where $q_i\in {\mathbb{A}}$.
    Let $i_0\in [d-1]_0$. For every $j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^+$, set $i_j$ to be $(i_{j-1}+m) \! \bmod \!\lambda$. Note that $i_j=i_0$ if and only if $\lambda$ divides $jm$.
    So the minimal element in $\{j \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+} \mid i_j=i_0\}$ is equal to $\mu$.
   In other words, $i_0,\ldots,i_{\mu-1}$ are pairwise distinct.
   Moreover, $i_1,\ldots,i_{\mu-1}$ strictly lie between $d-1$ and $\lambda$. By the construction of $i_j$, for every $j \in [\mu-1]$, the coefficient  of $t^{i_j}$ in $\Delta_f^{(\ell)}(q)$ is  $s\tilde\sigma(q_{i_{j-1}}) a^{i_{j-1}} - q_{i_j}$. 
   And $s\tilde\sigma(q_{i_{\mu-1}}) a^{i_{\mu-1}} - q_{i_0}$ is the coefficient  of $t^{i_0}$ in $\Delta_{f}^{(\ell)}(q)$, denoted by $w$. Clearly, $w\in \operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i,\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)})$.
   Since $g$ is spanned by $1,t,\ldots,t^{d-1}$, $q_{i_j}=s\tilde\sigma(q_{i_{j-1}}) a^{i_{j-1}}$ for every $j \in [\mu-1]$. Replacing $i$, $u_0$ with $i_0$ and $q_{i_{0}}$  in Lemma~\ref{LM:FormularForKshift}~(ii), respectively, we get $s\tilde\sigma(q_{i_{\mu-1}}) a^{i_{\mu-1}}=p_{i_0,\mu}\tilde\sigma^\mu(q_{i_0})$. 
   It follows that 
   $w=p_{i_0,\mu}\tilde\sigma^\mu(q_{i_0}) - q_{i_0}=\Delta^{(\mu\ell)}_{p_{i_0,\mu}}(q_{i_0})$. Hence, $w=0$
   by $w \in \operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{i,\mu}}^{(\mu\ell)}) \cap \operatorname{im} (\Delta^{(\mu\ell)}_{p_{i_0,\mu}})=\{0\}$. That means, for every $i_0\in[d-1]_0$, the coefficient of $t^{i_0}$ in $g$ must be equal to zero. Consequently, $g=0$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- alg-2: body_md -->
{\tt CRForSimpleR-Extensions}$({\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle,g,f,\ell)$\label{ALG:Rcase}
	
	\noindent
	{\tt Input:} The data in Convention \ref{CON:RCase}, $g\in {\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$; we assume that $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable and Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR} is constructive.
	
	\noindent
	{\tt Output:} A $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_{f}^{(\ell)}$ as given in Lemma~\ref{LM:ComplementforRExt}.
    
    \noindent
   {\tt Remark:} Set $p_{i, j}:=(sa^i)^{\sigma,j}(a^m)^{\sigma,\sigma,j}$ for every $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}$ and $j\in {\mathbb{N}}$ in the following pseudo-code.
	
	\smallskip \noindent
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item[(1)] $d \leftarrow \gcd(\lambda,m)$, $\mu \leftarrow\lambda/ d$, $(q, r) \leftarrow 0,0$
		\item[(2)] {\tt for} $i$ {\tt from} $0$ {\tt to} $\lambda-1$ {\tt do} 
		\begin{itemize}
			\item []  $i'\leftarrow i \bmod  d$, \, $u \leftarrow \operatorname{coeff}(g,t,i)$, \, 
			\item [] compute $k\in {\mathbb{N}}$ s.t.\ $i'= (i+km) \bmod \lambda$ 
            \item [] $(q, r') \leftarrow q-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i,j}\tilde{\sigma}^j(u)t^{(i+jm) \! \bmod \!\lambda},\, p_{i,k}\tilde{\sigma}^k(u)$
			\item [] $(v, w) \leftarrow$ \texttt{CRForGroundRing}(${\mathbb{A}},r',p_{i', \mu},\ell$),
            \item [] $(q, r) \leftarrow q+\sum_{j=0}^{\mu-1}p_{i',j}\tilde{\sigma}^j(v)t^{(i'+jm) \! \bmod \!\lambda},\,  r+wt^{i'}$
		\end{itemize} 
		{\tt end do}
		\item[(3)] {\tt return} $(q, r)$	
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-2 -->
<!-- example-5: body_md -->
\label{EX:Rmonomials}
 Let ${\mathbb{A}}:=C(x)$ with $\sigma(x)=x+1$, ${G}=C(x)^*$ and $t$ be an $R$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ with $\sigma(t)=-t$. For $f=-t$ and $g=t/x$, we compute a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_f$. Note that $\lambda=2$,  $\ell=m=1$, $s=a=-1$, $\tilde{\sigma}=\sigma$ as in Convention \ref{CON:RCase}. It follows that $d=1$, $\mu=2$ and $p_{0, \mu}=-1$ as given in Lemma~\ref{LM:ComplementforRExt}. Moreover, ${\mathbb{A}} \langle t\rangle=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f)\oplus \operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{0,2}}^{(2)})$. By Lemma \ref{LM:FormularForKshift}, $g=\Delta_{f}(-t/x)+r$, where $r=1/(x+1)$. Moreover, $r \in \operatorname{im}(\phi_{p_{0,2}}^{(2)})$ by Example~\ref{EX:rational}. So $(-t/x, 1/(x+1))$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_f$.
<!-- end example-5 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{Prop:NestRExt}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a computable difference ring with constant field $C$
and $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}_n:={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1\rangle \ldots \langle t_n\rangle$ be a simple $R$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. 
Assume that there is an algorithm which, for every $z\in {\mathbb{A}}^*$ and $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, constructs a complete reduction $\phi_z^{(i)}$ for $({\mathbb{A}},\,\Delta_{z}^{(i)})$ (viewed as a $C$-linear map). Then there is an algorithm that,
for every $f \in {({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_n}$ and $\ell \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, constructs a complete reduction $\psi_f^{(\ell)}$ as a $C$-linear map for $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\, \Delta_f^{(\ell)})$.
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We proceed by induction on $n$. It is evident for the case $n=0$.
Assume that, for every $z \in {({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_{n-1}}$ and $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, one can construct a complete reduction $\phi_{n-1,z}^{(i)}$ as a $C$-linear map for $({\mathbb{E}}_{n-1},\, \Delta_z^{(i)})$ and assume that the map in Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR} is defined by $(z,i)\rightarrow \phi_{n-1,z}^{(i)}$. Since $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\sigma)$ is a simple $R$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$, ${({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_{n-1}}$ is closed under $\sigma$ and $\sigma(t_n)/t_n \in {({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_{n-1}}$. Moreover, ${({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_{n}}=\{st_n^m~|~s\in {({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_{n-1}}~\text{and}~m\in [\lambda-1]_0\}$ with $\operatorname{ord}(t_n)=\lambda$. For every $f\in {({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_{n}}$ and $\ell \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$,  a complete reduction $\psi_{f}^{(\ell)}$ for $({\mathbb{E}}_n, \Delta_f^{(\ell)})$ is  obtained from Lemma~\ref{LM:ComplementforRExt}. The map in Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:indR} is then defined by $(f, \ell) \mapsto \psi_{f}^{(\ell)}$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- alg-3: body_md -->
\label{ALG:CRForSimpleR-Towers}
{\tt CRForSimpleR-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}}_n, g, f,\ell)$

 \noindent
{\tt Input:} A simple R-tower $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}_n:=C(x)\langle t_1\rangle \ldots \langle t_n\rangle$ over the rational difference field $(C(x),\,\sigma)$, $f\in (C(x)^*)_{C(x)}^{{\mathbb{E}}_n}$, $g \in{\mathbb{E}}_n$ and $\ell \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$; we assume that $C$ is factorizable.

\noindent
{\tt Output:} A $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_{f}^{(\ell)}$ as given in Proposition~\ref{Prop:NestRExt}.
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[]  {\tt if} $n=0$ {\tt then} 
\begin{itemize}
  \item [] {\tt return} {\tt CRForRationalFunctions}$(C(x),g, f, \ell)$ (*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForRationalFunctions}*)
\end{itemize}
\item[] {\tt else}
 \begin{itemize}
   \item[] {\tt return}  {\tt CRForSimpleR-Extensions}$({\mathbb{E}}_{n-1}\langle t_n \rangle, g, f,\ell)$
   \item[]\hspace*{1.2cm}\begin{minipage}{9cm} where  the command \texttt{CRForGroundRing} is replaced by \texttt{CRForSimpleR-Towers} (*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:Rcase}*)
   \end{minipage}  
   
 \end{itemize}
\item[]  {\tt end if}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-3 -->
<!-- convention-3: body_md -->
\label{CON:PiCase}
In this section, let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring with constant field $C$, and ${G}$ be a multiplicative subgroup of ${\mathbb{A}}^*$. Let $t$ be a $\Pi$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ with $a:=\sigma(t)/t \in {G}$. Set $f=st^m$, where $s\in G$ and $m\in {\mathbb{Z}}$.
<!-- end convention-3 -->
<!-- hyp-2: body_md -->
\label{HYP:PiInd}
We assume that there is a map that assigns to each element $z$ of ${G}$ 
a complete reduction $\phi_z$ for $({\mathbb{A}},\, \Delta_z)$.
<!-- end hyp-2 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{LM:PiFormularForKshift}
    Let $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}$ and $u_0:=u\in {\mathbb{A}}$. Set $p_{i,j}:=(sa^i)^{\sigma,j}(a^m)^{\sigma,\sigma,j}$
    for every $j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^+$. Then, for every $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$,  there is  $v_i=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i,j}\sigma^j(u)t^{i+jm} \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$ such that
    \begin{equation*}\label{EQ:image}
    p_{i,k}\sigma^k(u) t^{i+km}= ut^i+\Delta_f(v_i).
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The proof is analogous to that of Lemma~\ref{LM:FormularForKshift}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{LM:m>0}
If $m>0$, then ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \oplus {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}.$ In particular, for $g \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$, one can explicitly compute $q \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$ such that $g-\Delta_{f}(q)\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Let $i\in{\mathbb{Z}}$ and $u\in {\mathbb{A}}$. First we show that $ut^i \in \operatorname{im} (\Delta_f)+ {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{< m}$. If $i \in [m-1]_0$, $ut^i \in {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$. Set $p_{i,j}:=(sa^i)^{\sigma,j}(a^m)^{\sigma,\sigma,j}$
    for every $j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^+$. If $i<0$, then there is $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $i'\in [m-1]_0$  such that $i'=i+km$.
    By Lemma~\ref{LM:PiFormularForKshift}, we can take  $v=-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i,j}\sigma^{j}(u)t^{i+jm}$ such that
    \[ut^i-\Delta_f(v)=p_{i,k}\sigma^k(u) t^{i'}\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{< m}.\]
    Assume that $i\ge m$.
    Then there are $i'\in [m-1]_0$ and $k\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ such that $i'+km=i$. 
    Note that $p_{i',k} \in {\mathbb{A}}^*$ by $s, a\in G$. 
   Replacing $i$ and $u$ in Lemma~\ref{LM:PiFormularForKshift} with $i'$ and $\sigma^{-k}\left(u/p_{i',k}\right)$, respectively, we can take  $v=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i',j}\sigma^{j-k}\left(\frac{u}{p_{i', k}}\right)t^{i'+jm}$ 
   such that
    \[ u t^{i}-\Delta_f(v)=\sigma^{-k}\left(\frac{u}{p_{i',k}}\right)t^{i'}\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{< m}.\]

Let $g\in{\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$. We decompose $g$ as $g = g^{+}+g^{-}$, where $g^{+}\in{\mathbb{A}}[t]$ and $g^{-}\in t^{-1}{\mathbb{A}}[t^{-1}]$. 
Based on the above analysis, we can construct $q^{-} \in t^{-1}{\mathbb{A}}[t^{-1}]$ and $r^{-} \in {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$ such that  $g^{-}=\Delta_f(q^{-})+r^{-}$. 
Similarly, we can construct $q^{+} \in {\mathbb{A}}[t]$ and $r^{+} \in {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$ such that $g^{+}=\Delta_f(q^{+})+r^{+}$. 
Set $q:=q^{+}+q^{-}\in{\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$ and $r:=r^{+}+r^{-}\in{\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$. 
Then $g=\Delta_f(q)+r$, which implies that ${\mathbb{A}}[ t,t^{-1}]=\operatorname{im} (\Delta_f)+ {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{< m}$. 
 
It remains to show that $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \cap {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}=\{0\}$. Suppose that $p \in \operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \cap {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$ is nonzero. Then there is $q \in {\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$ such that $p=\Delta_f(q)$.  
We arrange $q$ in descending degree, and write it as $q_{k}t^{k}+\cdots+q_{\ell}t^{\ell}$, where $q_{\ell}, q_k \in {\mathbb{A}}$ with $q_{\ell}q_k \neq 0$.
Since $m>0$, $\operatorname{tdeg}_t(\Delta_f(q))=\ell$. So $\operatorname{tdeg}_t(q)=\operatorname{tdeg}_t(\Delta_f(q))$, which implies that $q\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]$ by $\Delta_f(q)=p\in {\mathbb{A}}[t]$.
Moreover, the leading term of $\Delta_f(q)$ is equal to $sa^k\sigma(q_k)t^{m+k}$ and $k\ge 0$. 
 Then $sa^k\sigma(q_k)=0$ by $\deg_t(p)<m$. 
 Note that $s, a \in {\mathbb{A}}^*$ and $\sigma$ is an automorphism. 
 So $q_k=0$, which implies that $q=0$, a contradiction. 
 Consequently, ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \oplus {\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}.$
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{LM:m<0}
If $m<0$, then ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \oplus t^m{\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<-m}.$ In particular, for $g \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$, one can explicitly compute $q \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$  
such that $g-\Delta_{f}(q)\in t^m{\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<-m}$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{LM:m=0}
If $m=0$, then ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \oplus (\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}{\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i}).$ In particular, for every $g \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$, one can construct $q \in {\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$ 
such that $g-\Delta_{f}(q)\in \sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}{\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i}$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Since $m=0$,  
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:m=0}
\Delta_f(ut^i)=(sa^i\sigma(u)-u)t^i 
\end{equation}
for every $u \in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}$.
 Let $g=\sum_{i\in {\mathbb{Z}}}g_it^i$, where $g_i \in {\mathbb{A}}$, finitely many nonzero. Note that $s, a \in G$ and $G$ is a multiplicative group. So $sa^i\in G$. By Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:PiInd}, $g_i=\Delta_ {sa^i}(u_i)+\phi_{sa^i}(g_i)$ for some $u_i \in {\mathbb{A}}$, which, together with \eqref{EQ:m=0}, implies that $g_it^i=\Delta_f(u_i t^i)+\phi_{sa^i}(g_i)t^i$. Set $q:=\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}} u_it^i$ and $r=\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}\phi_{sa^i}(g_i)t^i$. Then $g=\Delta_f(q)+r$.
 So ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}] = \operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) +(\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}{\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i})$. 
  Finally, we show that $\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \cap (\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i)=\{0\}$. Let $p\in \operatorname{im}(\Delta_f) \cap (\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i)$. Then there is $v \in {\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$ such that $p=\Delta_f(v)$. Write $p, v$ as $\sum_{i\in {\mathbb{Z}}}p_it^i, \sum_{i\in {\mathbb{Z}}} v_it^i$, where $p_i, v_i \in {\mathbb{A}}$, finitely many nonzero, respectively. 
 Then $p=\sum_{i\in {\mathbb{Z}}}(\Delta_{sa^i}(v_i)t^i)$, which, together with $p \in \sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i$, 
 implies that $p_i \in \operatorname{im}(\Delta_{sa^i})\cap \operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})$ for all $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}$. Hence, $p_i=0$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{LM:PiExt}
One can construct a $C$-subspace $W$ such that ${\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]=\operatorname{im}(\Delta_f)\oplus W$. Depending on whether $m>0$, $m<0$ or $m=0$,  $W$ is given respectively by ${\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<m}$, $t^m{\mathbb{A}}[t]_{<-m}$ or $\sum_{i \in {\mathbb{Z}}}{\operatorname{im}(\phi_{sa^i})t^i} $. Moreover, the projection from ${\mathbb{A}}[t, t^{-1}]$ onto $W$ is a complete reduction $\psi_f$ for $({\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}],\, \Delta_f)$. If $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable and Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:PiInd} is constructive, one can compute $\Sigma$-pairs for all elements in ${\mathbb{A}}[t,t^{-1}]$ with respect to $\psi_f$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- alg-4: body_md -->
{\tt CRForSimple$\Pi$-Extensions}$({\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle,g,f)$\label{ALG:Picase}

 \noindent
{\tt Input:} The data in Convention \ref{CON:PiCase}, $g\in {\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$; we assume that $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable and Hypothesis~\ref{HYP:PiInd} is constructive.

\noindent
{\tt Output:} A $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_{f}$ as given in Lemma~\ref{LM:PiExt}.

\noindent
{\tt Remark:} Set $p_{i, j}:=(sa^i)^{\sigma,j}(a^m)^{\sigma,\sigma,j}$ for every $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}$ and $j\in {\mathbb{N}}$ in the following pseudo-code.

\smallskip \noindent
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[(1)] $(d, \ell) \leftarrow \operatorname{hdeg}_t(g), \operatorname{tdeg}_t(g)$, $(q, r) \leftarrow 0, 0$

\item[(2)] {\tt if} $m>0$ {\tt then}
\begin{itemize}
  \item [] {\tt for} $i$ {\tt from} $\ell$ {\tt to} $d$ {\tt do}
  \begin{itemize}
    \item [] $u \leftarrow \operatorname{coeff}(g, t, i)$
    \item [] {\tt if} $0 \le i <m$ {\tt then} $r \leftarrow r+ut^i$ {\tt end if}
    \item [] {\tt if} $i<0$ {\tt then}
    \begin{itemize}
      \item []  compute $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $i' \in [m-1]_0$ s.t. $i'=i+km$
      \item [] $(q, r) \leftarrow q-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i,j}\sigma^{j}(u)t^{i+jm}, r+p_{i,k}\sigma^{k}(u)t^{i'}$
    \end{itemize}
     \item []{\tt end if}
    \item [] {\tt if} $i\ge m$ {\tt then}
    \begin{itemize}
      \item []  compute $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $i' \in [m-1]_0$ s.t. $i'+km=i$
      \item [] $(q, r) \leftarrow q+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i',j}\sigma^{j-k}\left(\frac{u}{p_{i', k}}\right)t^{i'+jm}, r+\sigma^{-k}\left(\frac{u}{p_{i', k}}\right)t^{i'}$
   \end{itemize}
    {\tt end if}
  \end{itemize}
   \item[] {\tt end do} 
\end{itemize}
{\tt end if}
\item[(3)] {\tt if} $m<0$ {\tt then}
\begin{itemize}
  \item [] {\tt for} $i$ {\tt from} $\ell$ {\tt to} $d$ {\tt do}
  \begin{itemize}
    \item [] $u \leftarrow \operatorname{coeff}(g, t, i)$
    \item [] {\tt if} $m < i \le 0$ {\tt then} $r \leftarrow r+ut^i$ {\tt end if}
    \item [] {\tt if} $i>0$ {\tt then}
    \begin{itemize}
      \item []  compute $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $m <i'\le 0 $ s.t. $i'=i+km$
      \item [] $(q, r) \leftarrow q-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i,j}\sigma^{j}(u)t^{i+jm}, r+p_{i,k}\sigma^{k}(u)t^{i'}$
    \end{itemize}
     \item []{\tt end if}
   \item [] {\tt if} $i\le m$ {\tt then}
    \begin{itemize}
      \item []  compute $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $m <i'\le 0$ s.t. $i'+km=i$
      \item [] $(q, r) \leftarrow q+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}p_{i',j}\sigma^{j-k}\left(\frac{u}{p_{i', k}}\right)t^{i'+jm}, r+\sigma^{-k}\left(\frac{u}{p_{i', k}}\right)t^{i'}$
    \end{itemize}
    {\tt end if}
  \end{itemize}
   \item[] {\tt end do} 
\end{itemize}
{\tt end if}
\item[(4)] {\tt if} $m=0$ {\tt then}
\begin{itemize}
  \item [] {\tt for} $i$ {\tt from}  $\ell$ {\tt to} $d$ {\tt do}
  \begin{itemize}
    \item [] $u \leftarrow \operatorname{coeff}(g, t, i)$
	\item [] $(v, w) \leftarrow$ \texttt{CRForGroundRing}(${\mathbb{A}},u,sa^i$)
    \item[] $(q, r) \leftarrow q+vt^i, r+wt^i$
    \end{itemize}
   \item[] {\tt end do} 
\end{itemize}
{\tt end if}
	\item[(5)] {\tt return} $(q, r)$
	
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-4 -->
<!-- example-6: body_md -->
\label{EX:RPiCase}
We will simplify the sum
$$\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{(1+k+2(-1)^kk)(-1)^{\lfloor \frac{k}{2} \rfloor}2^k}{k(1+k)}$$
as follows.
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{A}}:=C(x)\langle y_1\rangle \langle y_2\rangle$ be the $R$-tower over $(C(x),\,\sigma)$, where  $\sigma(y_1)=-y_1$ and $\sigma(y_2)=-y_1y_2$. Take a $\Pi$-monomial $t$ over ${\mathbb{A}}$ such that $\sigma(t)=2t$. Then $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}:={\mathbb{A}}\langle t\rangle$ forms a simple $R\Pi$-tower over $(C(x),\,\sigma)$. Moreover,
$y_1$, $y_2$ and $t$ model $(-1)^k$, $(-1)^{\lfloor \frac{k}{2} \rfloor}$ and $2^k$, respectively. 
 The given summand is represented by $g:=\tilde{g}t\in{\mathbb{E}}$  with $$\tilde{g}:=\frac{(1+x+2xy_1)y_2}{x(1+x)}.$$

We apply Algorithm~\ref{ALG:Picase} to compute a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_1$ as given in Lemma~\ref{LM:PiExt}. Note that the values in Convention \ref{CON:PiCase} are $a=2$,  $s=1$ and $m=0$.
So we enter step 4 of Algorithm~\ref{ALG:Picase} and 
it suffices to compute a $\Sigma$-pair of $\tilde{g}$ with respect to $\phi_2$ in ${\mathbb{A}}$, where $\phi_2$ is a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{A}},\, \Delta_2)$. Applying Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleR-Towers} to $\tilde{g}$ yields the $\Sigma$-pair $(-y_2/x, 0)$ of $\tilde{g}$  with respect to $\phi_2$. Hence, $(-y_2t/x, 0)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_1$. Rephrasing the arising variables to their corresponding summation objects, telescoping leads to the identity
 $$\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{(1+k+2(-1)^kk)(-1)^{\lfloor \frac{k}{2} \rfloor}2^k}{k(1+k)}=-\frac{(-1)^{\lfloor \frac{n+1}{2} \rfloor} 2^{n+1}}{n+1}-2.$$
<!-- end example-6 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{PROP:NestedPiCase}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a computable difference ring with constant field $C$, and $G$ be a multiplicative subgroup of ${\mathbb{A}}^*$.  Let 
 $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}_n:={\mathbb{A}}\langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_n \rangle$ be a $G$-simple $\Pi$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$.  Assume that there is an algorithm which, for every $z\in G$, constructs a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{A}},\, \Delta_z)$. Then there is an algorithm that, for every $f\in {G}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}_n}$, constructs a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\, \Delta_f)$.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{PROP:NestedRPiExt}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a computable difference ring with constant field $C$ and
 $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ be a simple $R\Pi$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$.
Assume that there is an algorithm which, for every $z\in {\mathbb{A}}^*$ and $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, constructs a complete reduction $\phi_z^{(i)}$  for $({\mathbb{A}},\,\Delta_{z}^{(i)})$ as a $C$-linear map. Then there is an algorithm that, for every $f\in {({\mathbb{A}}^*)}_{{\mathbb{A}}}^{{\mathbb{E}}}$, constructs a complete reduction $\rho_f$ for $({\mathbb{E}},\, \Delta_f)$.
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- alg-5: body_md -->
\label{ALG:CRForSimpleRPi-Towers}
{\tt CRForSimpleR$\Pi$-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}}, g, f)$

 \noindent
{\tt Input:} A simple $R\Pi$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}:=C(x)\langle y_1 \rangle \langle y_2 \rangle \ldots \langle y_{\ell_1} \rangle \langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_{\ell_2} \rangle$ over $(C(x),\,\sigma)$,  where the $y_i$ are R-monomials and $p_i$ are $\Pi$-monomials, $f\in {(C(x)^*)}_{C(x)}^{{\mathbb{E}}}$ and $g \in{\mathbb{E}}$; $C$ is factorizable.

\noindent
{\tt Output:} A $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\rho_{f}$ in Proposition~\ref{PROP:NestedRPiExt}.
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[]  {\tt if} $\ell_2=0$ {\tt then} 
\begin{itemize}
  \item [] {\tt return} {\tt CRForSimple$R$-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}}, g, f, 1)$ (*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleR-Towers}*)
\end{itemize}
\item[] {\tt else}
 \begin{itemize}
   \item[] {\tt return}  {\tt CRForSimple$\Pi$-Extensions}$(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}\langle p_{\ell_2} \rangle, g, f)$(*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:Picase}*)\\
   \hspace*{1.2cm}\begin{minipage}{9cm} with $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}:=C(x)\langle y_1 \rangle \langle y_2 \rangle \ldots \langle y_{\ell_1} \rangle \langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_{\ell_2-1}\rangle$,
   	where the command \texttt{CRForGroundRing} is replaced \\ by \texttt{CRForSimple$R\Pi$-Towers} (*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPi-Towers}*)
   \end{minipage}
 \end{itemize}
\item[]  {\tt end if}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-5 -->
<!-- define-8: body_md -->
\label{Def:effectiveBasis}
    Let $A$ be a ring with a subfield $C$, $\Theta$ be a $C$-basis of $A$ and $\theta  \in \Theta  $. Define $\theta^*$ to be the $C$-linear function on $A$ that maps $\theta$ to $1$ and all other elements to $0$. For a nonzero element $a$ of $A$, we say that $\theta$ is {\em effective} for $a$ if $\theta^*(a) \neq 0$. Furthermore,  $\Theta$ is called an {\em effective $C$-basis} if there are two algorithms available:
\begin{itemize}
\item [(i)]~given a nonzero element $a\in A$, find $\theta \in \Theta$ with $\theta^*(a)\neq0$; and 
\item [(ii)]~given $\theta \in \Theta  $ and $a \in A$, compute $\theta^*(a)$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end define-8 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{LM:SigmaExt}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ be a difference ring with constant field $C$ and  $({\mathbb{A}}[t],\, \sigma)$ be a $\Sigma^*$-extension of ${\mathbb{A}}$. Assume that $\phi$ is a complete reduction  for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$.
 Let $\Theta$ be a $C$-basis of ${\mathbb{A}}$ and $\theta \in \Theta$ be effective for $\phi(\Delta(t))$. Define
$$V_{\theta}=\sum_{i\in {\mathbb{N}}} (\operatorname{im}(\phi) \cap \ker(\theta^*)) t^i.$$
Then ${\mathbb{A}}[t]=\operatorname{im}(\Delta)\oplus V_{\theta}$. In particular, the projection from ${\mathbb{A}}[t]$ onto $V_{\theta}$ is a complete reduction $\psi_{\theta}$ for $({\mathbb{A}}[t],\, \Delta)$.
If $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is computable, $\Theta$ is effective and one can compute a $\Sigma$-pair for any element in ${\mathbb{A}}$ with respect to $\phi$, then one can compute $\Sigma$-pairs for all elements in ${\mathbb{A}}[t]$ with respect to  $\psi_{\theta}$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- alg-6: body_md -->
{\tt CRFor$\Sigma^*$-Extensions}$({\mathbb{A}}[ t],g)$\label{ALG:Sigmacase}
	
	\noindent
	{\tt Input:} A $\Sigma^*$-monomial $t$ over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ with constant field $C$ and $g\in {\mathbb{A}}[ t]$; we assume that computing $\Sigma$-pairs with respect to a complete reduction $\phi$ for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$ is constructive for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$ and ${\mathbb{A}}$ has an effective $C$-basis.
    
	\noindent
	{\tt Output:} a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi_{\theta}$ given in Lemma~\ref{LM:SigmaExt}.
<!-- end alg-6 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{PROP:SigmaExt}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a computable difference ring with constant field $C$ and $({\mathbb{E}}_n,\, \sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}_n:={\mathbb{A}}[t_1, \ldots,t_n]$ be a $\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$. Assume that $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ has an effective $C$-basis and there exists an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\phi$ for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$. Then there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\psi$ for $({\mathbb{E}}_n, \, \Delta)$.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{THM:SimpleRPiSigmaExt}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a computable difference ring with constant field $C$ and $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ be a simple $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$. Assume that ${\mathbb{A}}$ has an effective $C$-basis $\Theta_0$, and there is an algorithm that, for every $f \in {\mathbb{A}}^*$ and $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, constructs a complete reduction $\phi_f^{(i)}$ for $({\mathbb{A}},\, \Delta_f^{(i)})$. Then there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\psi$ for $({\mathbb{E}}, \, \Delta)$.
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
By \cite[Lemma~4.10]{Schn2016}, ${\mathbb{E}}$ can be reordered as~\eqref{Equ:ReorderedSimpleExt},
where the $y_i$ are $R$-monomials, the $p_i$ are $\Pi$-monomials, and the $s_i$ are $\Sigma^*$-monomials.
Set ${\mathbb{E}}_1$ to be ${\mathbb{A}} \langle y_1\rangle \ldots \langle y_{\ell_1} \rangle \langle  p_1\rangle \ldots \langle p_{\ell_2} \rangle$.
  Then there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\rho$ for $({\mathbb{E}}_1, \Delta)$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:NestedRPiExt}.
Note that ${\mathbb{E}}_1$ has an effective $C$-basis
$$\Theta=\{ \theta_0 y_1^{k_1}\cdots y_{\ell_1}^{k_{\ell_1}} p_1^{i_1}\cdots p_{\ell_2}^{i_{\ell_2}}~|~\theta_0 \in \Theta_0,~k_j \in[\operatorname{ord}(y_j)-1]_0~~\text{and}~i_j \in {\mathbb{Z}}\}$$
and $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is a $\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{E}}_1,\sigma)$. So there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\psi$ for $({\mathbb{E}}, \, \Delta)$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:SigmaExt}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{COR:RPiSigmaExt+Rational}
Let $(C(x), \, \sigma)$ be the rational difference field with $\sigma(x)=x+1$, where $C$ is factorizable. And let $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ be a simple $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $(C(x), \, \sigma^k)$ for some $k \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$.
Then there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\psi$ for $({\mathbb{E}},\, \Delta)$.
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Note that $C(x)$ is constant-stable. So $\operatorname{const}(C(x), \sigma^{k})=C$. Further, there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\phi^{(ki)}_f$ for $(C(x), \Delta^{(ki)}_f)$ for every $f\in C(x)^*$ and 
 $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:Generalrational+highorder}. Set $X = \left\{ x^i \mid i \in {\mathbb{N}} \right\}$ and
$M_{x}$ to be the set consisting of all monic and irreducible polynomials in $C[x]$ with positive degrees. Using irreducible partial fraction decomposition, we obtain that
\begin{equation} \label{EQ:basis0}
  \Theta_0 =  X \cup \left\{ \frac{x^i}{q^j} \mid q \in M_x, 0 \le i < \deg_x(q), j \in {\mathbb{Z}}^+ \right\}
\end{equation}
is an effective $C$-basis of $C(x)$.
Then the theorem follows from Theorem~\ref{THM:SimpleRPiSigmaExt}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- alg-7: body_md -->
\label{ALG:CRForSimpleRPiSigma-Towers}
	{\tt CRForSimple$R\Pi\Sigma^*$-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}}, g)$
	
	\noindent
	{\tt Input:} A simple $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}:={\mathbb{A}}[s_1, \ldots, s_n]$ over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$,  where $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is an R$\Pi$-tower over $(C(x),\,\sigma)$ and $C$ is factorizable (place the R-monomials first, followed by the $\Pi$-monomials), and the $s_i$ are $\Sigma^*$-monomials, and $g \in{\mathbb{E}}$.
	
	\noindent
	{\tt Output:} A $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\psi$ as given in Theorem \ref{COR:RPiSigmaExt+Rational}.
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item[]  {\tt if} $n=0$ {\tt then} 
		\begin{itemize}
			\item [] {\tt return} {\tt CRForSimple$R\Pi$-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}}, g, 1)$ (*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPi-Towers}*)
		\end{itemize}
		\item[] {\tt else}
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[] {\tt return}  {\tt CRFor$\Sigma^*$-Extensions}$({\mathbb{A}}[s_1,\ldots, s_{n-1}][s_n], g)$(*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:Sigmacase}*)\\  
			\hspace*{1.2cm}\begin{minipage}{9.5cm} where $\Sigma$-pairs with respect to a complete reduction $\phi$ for $({\mathbb{A}}[s_1,\ldots, s_{n-1}], \, \Delta)$ are computed by Algorithm {\tt CRForSimple$R\Pi\Sigma^*$-Towers} (*Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPiSigma-Towers}*)
			\end{minipage} 
		\end{itemize}
		\item[]  {\tt end if}
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-7 -->
<!-- example-7: body_md -->
\label{EX:RPiSigmaTowers}
We simplify the sum 
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n} (-1)^k \sum_{i=0}^{k} \binom{\nu}{i}$$
as follows. We start with $(C(x),\,\sigma)$, where $\sigma(x)=x+1$ and
$C={\mathbb{Q}}(\nu)$ is the rational function field in the variable $\nu$. Then we define the simple $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with 
${\mathbb{E}}:={\mathbb{Q}}(\nu)(x)\langle y \rangle \langle p \rangle \langle s \rangle$, and
$$\sigma(y)=-y,\quad \sigma(p)=\frac{\nu-x}{x+1}p \quad \text{and} \quad \sigma(s)=s+\frac{(\nu-x)p}{x+1}.$$
Here $y$ is an $R$-monomial representing $(-1)^k$, $p$ is a $\Pi$-monomial  denoting the binomial coefficient $\binom{\nu}{k}$, and $s$ is a $\Sigma^*$-monomial representing $\sum_{i=0}^{k} \binom{\nu}{i}$. 
So the given summand can be expressed as $y\,t$ in ${\mathbb{E}}$. Let $\psi$ be the complete reduction for $({\mathbb{E}}, \, \Delta)$ as given in Theorem~\ref{COR:RPiSigmaExt+Rational}. Employing Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPiSigma-Towers} to $y\,t$ yields a $\Sigma$-pair $\left(-\frac{ys}{2}+\frac{y(\nu-x)p}{2\nu}, 0\right)$
of $y\,t$ with respect to $\psi$. Reinterpreting the result in terms of summation objects, we obtain by telescoping the simplification
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n} (-1)^k \sum_{i=0}^{k} \binom{\nu}{i}=\frac{1}{2}(-1)^n\sum_{i=0}^{n+1} \binom{\nu}{i}+\frac{(-1)^n(n-\nu+1)\binom{\nu}{n+1}}{2\nu}.$$
<!-- end example-7 -->
<!-- define-9: body_md -->
\label{DEF:idempotent}
Let $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ be a difference ring and $\lambda \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$. If there exist idempotent, pairwise orthogonal elements $e_0,e_1,\ldots,e_{\lambda-1}$ of ${\mathbb{A}}$ (i.e., $e_u^2=e_u$ and $e_ue_v=0$ if $u\neq v$)
such that $\sigma(e_u)=e_{u+1 \! \bmod \! \lambda}$, $e_0+e_1+\cdots+e_{\lambda-1}=1$, then $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ is called a {\em weak idempotent difference ring  
with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in [\lambda-1]_0\}$}. 
If this is the case,  we have the following decomposition
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:idempotentrepresentation}
  {\mathbb{A}}=e_0{\mathbb{A}}\oplus e_1{\mathbb{A}} \oplus \cdots \oplus e_{\lambda-1}{\mathbb{A}}
\end{equation}
as a direct sum of ideals by \cite[Chapter~1, Exercise 1.7]{Lam2001}, which is called a {\em weak idempotent representation} of ${\mathbb{A}}$. 
In addition,
if for every $i \in [\lambda-1]_0$, $e_i{\mathbb{A}}$ is an integral domain, we call $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$ an {\em idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in [\lambda-1]_0\}$} and \eqref{EQ:idempotentrepresentation} is called an {\em idempotent representation} of ${\mathbb{A}}$.
<!-- end define-9 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{RE:idemp+unit}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be a weak idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in [\lambda-1]_0\}$. For every $s \in [\lambda-1]_0$,
\begin{itemize}
  \item [(i)] $(e_s{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$  is a difference ring with multiplicative identity $e_s$.
  \item [(ii)] The map $\sigma$ induces a difference ring isomorphism between $(e_s{\mathbb{A}}, \sigma^{\lambda})$ and $(e_{s+1}{\mathbb{A}}, \sigma^{\lambda})$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be an idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$ as given in \eqref{EQ:idempotentrepresentation}. Assume that $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ is also an idempotent difference ring with respect to  $\{f_i~|~i \in [\mu-1]_0\}$.
Then $\lambda=\mu$ and there is $j\in [\lambda-1]_0$  such that $e_i = f_{(i+j)\! \bmod \! \lambda}$ for all $i$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We say that a nonzero idempotent element of ${\mathbb{A}}$ is primitive if it can not be written as a sum of two  non-zero orthogonal idempotent elements of ${\mathbb{A}}$.  Suppose that $e_0$ as given in \eqref{EQ:idempotentrepresentation} is not primitive. Then there are  two nonzero orthogonal idempotent elements $r_1, r_2 \in {\mathbb{A}}$ such that $e_0=r_1+r_2$. It follows that $e_0{\mathbb{A}} \subset r_1{\mathbb{A}}+r_2{\mathbb{A}}$. For arbitrary $x,y \in {\mathbb{A}}$, $r_1x+r_2y=e_0(r_1x+r_2y)$ because  $r_1r_2=0$ and $r_i^2=r_i$ for $i=1,2$. So $e_0{\mathbb{A}}=r_1{\mathbb{A}} \oplus r_2 {\mathbb{A}}$, which implies that $r_1 \in e_0{\mathbb{A}}$. 
In particular, $r_1$ is an idempotent of $e_0{\mathbb{A}}$. 
Since $e_0{\mathbb{A}}$ is an integral domain, $e_0$ is the only nontrivial idempotent. In other words, $r_1=e_0$.
 Hence, $r_2=0$, a contradiction. 
 Similarly, the $e_i$, $f_i$ are primitive. 
 Note that $1=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}e_i=\sum_{i=0}^{\mu-1}f_i$. 
 So $\lambda=\mu$ and there is a permutation $\tau$ of $\{0, \ldots, \lambda-1\}$ such that $e_i = f_{\tau(i)}$ for all $i$ by \cite[Proposition~22.1]{Lam2001}. Thus, the conclusion holds by setting $j$ to be  $\tau(0)$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{LM:idemp+equivdef}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be a difference ring. Then it is a weak idempotent difference ring
if and only if there is an idempotent element $e_0$ of ${\mathbb{A}}$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:idempdecomp2}
  {\mathbb{A}}=e_0{\mathbb{A}} \oplus \sigma(e_0){\mathbb{A}} \oplus \cdots \oplus \sigma^{\lambda-1}(e_0) {\mathbb{A}}
\end{equation}
as a direct sum of ideals.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Let $\eqref{EQ:idempotentrepresentation}$ be a weak idempotent representation of ${\mathbb{A}}$. Since $\sigma(e_u)=e_{u+1 \! \bmod \! \lambda}$ for every $u \in [\lambda-1]_0$, we have $e_i=\sigma^{i}(e_0)$. So \eqref{EQ:idempdecomp2} holds. Conversely,  note that $e_0$ is idempotent, so are the $\sigma^i(e_0)$. For $i, j\in [\lambda-1]_0$ with $i \neq j$, $\sigma^i(e_0) \sigma^j(e_0)\in \sigma^{i}(e_0){\mathbb{A}} \cap \sigma^{j}(e_0){\mathbb{A}}=\{0\}$. Since $1\in {\mathbb{A}}$, there are $a_0, a_1, \ldots, a_{\lambda-1}\in {\mathbb{A}}$ such that $1=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(e_0)a_i$. Multiplying both sides of the above equation by $\sigma^{i}(e_0)$ yields $\sigma^{i}(e_0)=\sigma^{i}(e_0)a_i$, which implies that $1=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(e_0)$. Then applying $\Delta$  to the above equation gives 
$0=\sigma^{\lambda}(e_0)-e_0$. Hence, $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is a weak idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{\sigma^i(e_0)~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$ by Definition~\ref{DEF:idempotent}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{LM:anRmonomial+idem}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be a difference ring with constant field $C$. Assume further that 
$({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{A}}\langle y \rangle \langle t_1 \rangle \langle t_2 \rangle \ldots \langle t_k \rangle$ is an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$, where $y$ is an $R$-monomial over ${\mathbb{A}}$ with $\alpha:=\sigma(y)/y \in C$ of order $\lambda$. 
Set $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}={\mathbb{A}} \langle t_1 \rangle \langle t_2 \rangle \ldots \langle t_k \rangle$. Then the following assertions hold.
\begin{itemize}
  \item [(i)] One can compute 
  $e_0,e_1,\ldots,e_{\lambda-1}$ of $C\langle y \rangle$
 such that ${\mathbb{E}}$ is a weak idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$.
 \item [(ii)]  
  For every $s \in [\lambda-1]_0$ and $f\in{\mathbb{E}}$,  $e_sf=e_s\cdot(f|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}})$.
  In particular,
  $e_s{\mathbb{E}}=e_s\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$.
   \item[(iii)] For every $s\in [\lambda-1]_0$, $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_s)$  is an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$, where $\sigma_s$ is defined  by $\sigma_s(f)=\sigma^{\lambda}(f)|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$ for all $f \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. 
        In particular, for every $i\in [k]$, $t_i$ is an A-monomial over $({\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1\rangle\langle t_2\rangle \ldots \langle t_{i-1}\rangle,\, \sigma_s)$  of order $m_i$ if $t_i$ is an A-monomial over $({\mathbb{A}} \langle y\rangle\langle t_1\rangle \ldots \langle t_{i-1}\rangle,\, \sigma)$ of order $m_i$, and $t_i$ is a P/S-monomial over $({\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1\rangle \ldots \langle t_{i-1}\rangle,\, \sigma_s)$ if $t_i$ is a P/S-monomial over $({\mathbb{A}} \langle y\rangle\langle t_1\rangle \ldots \langle t_{i-1}\rangle,\, \sigma)$.
       
\item[(iv)] There is a difference ring isomorphism $\tau$ from $(e_s{\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ onto $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_s)$, mapping $e_sf$  to $f|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$ for every $f\in{\mathbb{E}}$, where $\sigma_s$ is given by (iii). Moreover, $\tau^{-1}(\tilde{f})=e_0\tilde{f}$ for every $\tilde{f} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$.
    
     \item[(v)]  Assume further that $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ is a $PS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}\langle y \rangle,\, \sigma)$ and ${\mathbb{A}}$ is an integral domain. Then  $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is an idempotent difference ring  with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$, where the $e_i$ are given by (i).
    
  \item [(vi)]  $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ if and only if there is $k\in [\lambda-1]_0$ such that $(\tilde {\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma_k)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^\lambda)$, where $\sigma_k$ is given by (iii). In this case, $\operatorname{const}(\tilde {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma_k)=  C$ if $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ is constant-stable.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Set $\alpha:=\sigma(y)/y$. 
Then $\alpha$ is a $\lambda$th primitive root of unity
by Theorem~\ref{THM:testingRPiSigma-monomials}~(iii). For every $s \in [\lambda-1]_0$, let
$$\tilde{e}_s(y):= \prod_{\substack{j=0 \\ j\neq \lambda-1-s}}^{\lambda-1} (y-\alpha^j).$$
Since $C$ is a field, $\tilde{e}_s(\alpha^{\lambda-1-s}) \neq 0$, which allows us to define
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:IdempEle}
e_s=e_s(y):=\frac{\tilde{e}_s(y)}{\tilde{e}_s(\alpha^{\lambda-1-s})} \in C\langle y \rangle \backslash \{0\}.
\end{equation}

(i)~$\alpha \in C$ implies $\sigma(e_s)=e_s(\alpha y)$. Moreover, $\sigma(e_s)=e_{s+1 \! \bmod \! \lambda}$ by a straightforward computation.
