It remains to show that the $e_i$ are pairwise orthogonal, idempotent, and sum to 1. Since $y^{\lambda}-1$ divides $e_i(y)e_j(y)$ for $i, j \in[\lambda-1]_0$ with $i\neq j$, the $e_i$ are pairwise orthogonal. On the other hand, write $e_s(y)^2-e_s(y)$ as $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}c_iy^i$ for $c_i \in C$. Let $C[z]$ be the ring of polynomials, where $z$ is an indeterminate over $C$. Consider $p_s(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}c_iz^i \in C[z]$.  Note that
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:idempotent}
e_s(\alpha^i)  =
\left\{
\begin{array}{ll}
1, &  \text{if $i=\lambda-1-s$}, \\ 
0,  & \text{if $i \neq \lambda-1-s$}.
\end{array} \right.
\end{equation}
So $p_s(\alpha^i)=0$ for every $s\in [\lambda-1]_0$.
Since $C$ is a field, $p_s$ has at most $\lambda-1$ zeros, which implies that $p_s$ must be zero.
 Hence, $e_s^2=e_s$. Similarly, we can show that $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}e_i=1$. 
 
(ii) First we show that the evaluation $\cdot~|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$ on ${\mathbb{E}}$ is well-defined. Let $f, g \in {\mathbb{E}}$ be such that $f-g=u(y^{\lambda}-1)$ for some $u \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}\langle y \rangle$. Then $f|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}=g |_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$ by $\alpha^{\lambda}=1$.
 Note that $f$ can be written as $(y-\alpha^{\lambda-1-s})\tilde f
+ f|_{y\to\alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$
for some $\tilde f\in\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[y]$.
Moreover, $e_s(y-\alpha^{\lambda-1-s})$ is a multiple of
$y^\lambda-1$, and hence vanishes. Therefore,
$e_sf= e_s(f|_{y\to\alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}).$

(iii) Set ${\mathbb{E}}_0={\mathbb{A}}\langle y\rangle$, ${\mathbb{E}}_i={\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}\langle t_i\rangle$, $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_0={\mathbb{A}}$ and $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_i=\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1}\langle t_i\rangle$ for $i \in [k]$. We proceed the assertion by induction on $i$. For the base case $i=0$, $\sigma_s(f)=\sigma^{\lambda}(f)$ for all $f\in {\mathbb{A}}$. Assume that $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1},\,\sigma_s)$ is an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}, \sigma^{\lambda})$. 
Consider $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1}\langle t_i\rangle, \,\sigma_s)$.
Let $\sigma(t_i)=a_it_i$ for some  $a_i \in {\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}^*$ if $t_i$ is an $AP$-monomial. 
By Lemma~\ref{LM:RPiSigma+term}~(ii), we have  $\sigma^{\lambda}(t_i)= a_i^{\sigma, \lambda}t_i$, which implies that
 $\sigma_s(t_i)=\tilde{a}_it_i$ for  $\tilde{a}_i=a_i^{\sigma, \lambda}~|_{y\rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$. 
Since $a_i \in {\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}^*$ and $\sigma$ is an automorphism, $\tilde{a}_i \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1}^*$. 
So $t_i$ is an $AP$-monomial over $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1},\, \sigma_s)$.
Furthermore, if $a_i$ is a $\lambda_i$th root of unity in ${\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}$, 
then $\tilde{a}_i$ is also a $\lambda_i$th root of unity in $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1}$.
That is, $t_i$ is an $A$-extension of $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1},\, \sigma_s)$ of order $\lambda_i$ if $t_i$ is an $A$-extension of $({\mathbb{E}}_{i-1},\, \sigma)$ of order $\lambda_i$. 
On the other hand,  $\sigma(t_i)=t_i+b_i$ for some $b_i \in {\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}$ if $t_i$ is a $\Sigma^*$-monomial. 
It follows from Lemma~\ref{LM:RPiSigma+term}~(i) that $\sigma_s(t_i)=t_i+\tilde{b}_i$ for some $\tilde{b}_i \in  {\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}$. 
So $t_i$ is an $S$-monomial over $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1}$. 
Consequently, $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}_i,\, \sigma_s)$ is an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$.

(iv)~By Remark \ref{RE:idemp+unit}~(i), $(e_sE, \, \sigma^{\lambda})$ is a difference ring. 
Next, we verify that $\tau$ is well-defined. Let $f, g \in{\mathbb{E}}$ be such that $e_sf=e_sg$. Then $e_s(f-g)=u(y^{\lambda}-1)$ for some $u \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}\langle y \rangle$. By \eqref{EQ:IdempEle}, $y-\alpha^{\lambda-1-s}$ divides $f-g$. So $f|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}=g |_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}$, which implies that $\tau$ is well defined. Furthermore, $\tau$ is a ring homomorphism from $e_s{\mathbb{E}}$ to $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. Now consider the ring homomorphism

\[
 \begin{array}{cccc}
\rho: & \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}} & \rightarrow & e_s{\mathbb{E}} \\
       &   \tilde{f}                & \mapsto     & e_s\tilde{f}.
\end{array}
\]
Then, for every $f\in{\mathbb{E}}$, $\rho(\tau(e_sf))=e_s(f|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}})=e_sf$ by (ii). 
On the other hand, $\tau(\rho(\tilde{f}))=\tilde{f}$ for every $\tilde{f} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. 
So $\rho \tau=\tau \rho=\bf{1}$. Thus, $\tau$ is a ring isomorphism and $\tau^{-1}=\rho$. 
By (ii) again, every element in $e_s{\mathbb{E}}$ can be expressed as $e_s \tilde{f}$ for some $\tilde{f} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. 
Then
\[\tau(\sigma^{\lambda}(e_s\tilde f))= \tau(e_s\sigma^{\lambda}(\tilde{f}))=\sigma^{\lambda}(\tilde f)|_{y \rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1-s}}=\sigma_s(\tilde f)=\sigma_s(\tau(e_s\tilde f)).\]
So $\tau \circ \sigma^{\lambda}=\sigma_s \circ \tau$. Consequently, $\tau$ is a difference ring isomorphism from  $(e_s{\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ onto $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_s)$.

(v) By (iii) and the assumption as given in (v), $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_s)$ is a $PS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. So $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ is an integral domain. Together with (iv), $e_s{\mathbb{E}}$ is also an integral domain. Therefore, by (i), ${\mathbb{E}}$ is an idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i \in [\lambda-1]_0\}$.

(vi) By (iii), $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_s)$ is an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ for every $s\in [\lambda-1]_0$. By (iv), it suffices to show that $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ if and only if there is $k\in[\lambda-1]_0$ such that $\operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})=\operatorname{const}(e_k{\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma^{\lambda})$.  
Assume that $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)=\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ and fix any $k\in[\lambda-1]_0$. Let $f \in {\mathbb{E}}$ be such that $e_kf \in \operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. Set $h$ to be $\sum_{j=0}^{\lambda-1} \sigma^j(e_kf)$. Then $\sigma(h)-h=\sigma^{\lambda}(e_kf)-e_kf=0$ by $e_kf \in \operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. So $h \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$, which implies that $h \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. 
Since $\sigma(e_k)=e_{k+1 \! \bmod \! \lambda}$, $e_{k}e_{k+1}=0$ and $e_k^2=e_k$, we have $e_kf=e_kh \in e_k{\mathbb{A}}$. So $e_k f \in \operatorname{const}(e_k{\mathbb{A}}, \,\sigma^{\lambda})$. 
Thus, $\operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})=\operatorname{const}(e_k{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ by $\operatorname{const}(e_k{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda}) \subset \operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. 
Conversely, suppose that we can choose $k\in[\lambda-1]_0$ such that $\operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})=\operatorname{const}(e_k{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$.
Let $f \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ and $s \in [\lambda-1]_0$.
Then $e_sf \in \operatorname{const}(e_s{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda})$. 
Since $\operatorname{const}(e_k {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})=\operatorname{const}(e_k{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$, we have $\operatorname{const}(e_s {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})=\operatorname{const}(e_s{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ by Remark \ref{RE:idemp+unit}. 
So $e_s f \in \operatorname{const}(e_s{\mathbb{A}}, \,\sigma^{\lambda})$. In particular, $e_sf \in {\mathbb{A}}\langle y \rangle$. This leads to
$$f=1\cdot f=e_0f+e_1f+\cdots+e_{\lambda-1}f \in {\mathbb{A}}\langle y \rangle.$$
Hence, $f \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}}\langle y \rangle,\, \sigma)$. Consequently, $f \in \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ because $y$ is an $R$-monomial. In this case, if $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is constant-stable, then $\operatorname{const}(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_s)= \operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})=C$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{PROP:checkingRPiSigmaTower}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ be a computable difference ring which is integral and constant-stable. Assume that, for every $k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, one can determine whether a $PS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^k)$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower.   Then one can determine whether an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower.
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Let $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1 \rangle \langle t_2 \rangle \ldots \langle t_e \rangle$ be an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. We proceed the conclusion by induction on the number $\ell$ of $A$-monomials in ${\mathbb{E}}$. The base case $\ell=0$ follows from the assumption of the proposition. Assume that $\ell >0$ and the conclusion holds for $\ell-1$. Let $j$ be the minimal such that $t_j$ is an $A$-monomial. Then $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\,\sigma)$ with $\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}={\mathbb{A}} \langle t_1 \rangle \langle t_2 \rangle \ldots \langle t_{j-1} \rangle$ is a $PS$-tower over  $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. 
By the assumption of the proposition, one can determine whether $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \sigma)$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$. If the answer is negative, $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is not an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$. Otherwise, $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \sigma)$ is constant-stable by Lemma~\ref{Lemma:ConstantStable}.  Set $\lambda=\operatorname{ord}(t_j)$. Then $t_j$ is an $R$-monomial over $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \sigma)$ if and only if $\sigma(t_j)/t_j$ is a primitive $\lambda$th root of unity in $C$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:R-monomial+primitive}. If the answer is negative, $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is not an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$. Otherwise, using Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem} (i), we obtain a weak idempotent representation of ${\mathbb{E}}$:
  $${\mathbb{E}}=e_0{\mathbb{E}} \oplus e_1{\mathbb{E}} \oplus \cdots \oplus e_{\lambda-1}{\mathbb{E}}.$$
  Set $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}:=\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}} \langle t_{j+1} \rangle \ldots \langle t_e \rangle$. By Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem} (vi) and Remark \ref{RE:idemp+unit}~(ii), $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \sigma)$ if and only if $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \sigma_0)$ with $\sigma_0(\tilde{f})=\sigma^{\lambda}(\tilde{f})|_{t_j\rightarrow \alpha^{\lambda-1}}$ for $\tilde{f} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. Recall that  $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ is constant-stable by Lemma~\ref{Lemma:ConstantStable}. Thus $(\tilde{{\mathbb{A}}}, \, \sigma^{\lambda})$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$, which implies that $({\mathbb{E}}, \, \sigma)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ if and only if $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \, \sigma_0)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$.\\ 
  Again we can determine whether a $PS$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower due to the assumption of the proposition. Since in the $APS$-tower
  $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \sigma_0)$ over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ there are only $\ell-1$ $A$-monomials left, we can apply the induction hypothesis to determine whether it is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower. If so, $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ is also an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- cor-1: body_md -->
\label{Cor:CheckInPiSigma}
Let $({\mathbb{F}},\,\sigma)$ be a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-field over an orbit-computable constant field $C$. Then one can determine whether an $APS$-tower over $({\mathbb{F}},\,\sigma)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower algorithmically.
<!-- end cor-1 -->
<!-- example-8: body_md -->
\label{EX:CheckRmonomials}
Let $({\mathbb{F}},\,\sigma)$ be the rational difference field with ${\mathbb{F}}=C(x)$ and $\sigma(x)=x+1$, where $C:={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1})$. 
By Remark~\ref{Remark:AlgRatOribt}, $C$ is orbit-computable. Take the $R$-monomial $y_1$ over ${\mathbb{F}}$ of order $2$ with $\sigma(y_1)=-y_1$. Let $e_0 :=\frac{1-y_1}{2}$ and $e_1 :=\frac{1+y_1}{2}$. Then $e_0, e_1$ are idempotent, pairwise orthogonal and $e_0+e_1=1$. Moreover, $e_0{\mathbb{F}} \oplus e_1{\mathbb{F}}$ is an idempotent representation of ${\mathbb{F}} \langle y_1 \rangle$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem}~(i). Set $w:=e_0+\sqrt{-1}e_1\in{\mathbb{F}}\langle y_1\rangle$. Then $w^4=1$. Let $y_2$ be an A-monomial of order $4$ with $\sigma(y_2)=wy_2$. Clearly, $y_2$ is not simple. 
Thus a check if it is an $R$-monomial cannot be carried out by the algorithms given in~\cite{Schn2016}. 
But it is possible by our new technique summarized in Corollary~\ref{Cor:CheckInPiSigma}. 
Consider $({\mathbb{F}}\langle y_2 \rangle,\, \sigma_0)$, where $\sigma_0(\tilde{f})=\sigma^2(\tilde{f})|_{y_1\rightarrow -1}$ for every $\tilde{f} \in {\mathbb{F}}\langle y_2 \rangle$. By a direct computation, $\sigma_0(y_2)=\sigma^2(y_2)|_{y_1\rightarrow -1}=\sqrt{-1}y_2$. Since $\sqrt{-1}$ is a $4$th primitive root of unity, $y_2$ is an $R$-monomial over $({\mathbb{F}},\,\sigma_0)$ and thus an $R$-extension over $({\mathbb{F}}\langle y_1\rangle,\sigma)$.
<!-- end example-8 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{PROP:RPiSigmaIsIdempotent}
Suppose that the difference ring $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ with constant field $C$ is integral and constant-stable. Let $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ with
${\mathbb{E}}:={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1 \rangle \langle t_2 \rangle \ldots \langle t_k \rangle$ be an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$, and set $${\mathbb{I}}:=\{ i \in [k]~|~t_i~\text{is an R-monomial}\}.$$ Assume further that ${\mathbb{I}}$ is not empty. Set $\lambda=\prod_{i\in {\mathbb{I}}} \operatorname{ord}(t_i)$. 
Then the following assertions hold.

\begin{itemize}
  \item [(i)]  One can compute $e_0,\ldots,e_{\lambda-1} \in {\mathbb{E}}$ such that $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is an idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i \mid i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$.
  \item [(ii)] Set ${\mathbb{H}} :={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_i~|~i\in [k] \backslash {\mathbb{I}} \rangle$. One can construct $\{\beta_i\in C \mid i\in {\mathbb{I}} \}$ such that $({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)$, where,  for every $h \in {\mathbb{H}}$, $\sigma_0(h)=\sigma^{\lambda}(h)|_{\{t_i \rightarrow \beta_i\mid i\in{\mathbb{I}}\}}$,  is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. Moreover, there is a difference ring isomorphism
      \[
\begin{array}{cccc}
\delta: & (e_0{\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^\lambda) & \rightarrow & ({\mathbb{H}}, \, \sigma_0) \\
      & e_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{\{t_i \rightarrow \beta_i \mid i\in{\mathbb{I}}\}}
\end{array}
\]
for every $f \in {\mathbb{E}}$. Furthermore, $\delta^{-1}(h)=e_0h$ for every $h \in {\mathbb{H}}$.     
\end{itemize}
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{PROP:orderRPiSigma}, ${\mathbb{E}}$ can be reordered as~\eqref{EQ:reoder+RPiSigma}
where the $y_i$ are $R$-monomials, the $p_i$ are $\Pi$-monomials, and the $s_i$ are $\Sigma^*$-monomials. Since ${\mathbb{I}}\neq\emptyset$, it follows that $\ell>0$.
Then $\lambda$ and ${\mathbb{H}}$ as given in the proposition are equal to $\prod_{i=1}^{\ell} \operatorname{ord}(y_i)$ and ${\mathbb{A}}\langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_{m} \rangle  \langle s_1 \rangle \ldots \langle s_n \rangle$, respectively.
We proceed the conclusion by induction on $\ell$. 
First we have $\sigma(y_1)/y_1 \in C$ by Proposition~\ref{PROP:R-monomial+primitive}. Set $\operatorname{ord}(y_{1})$  to be $\lambda_1$. 
According to Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem} (i), there are 
$u_0, u_1, \ldots, u_{\lambda_1-1} \in C \langle y \rangle$ such that $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is a weak idempotent difference ring with respect to 
$\{u_i\mid i\in[\lambda_1-1]_0\}$.

Let $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}:={\mathbb{A}}\langle y_2 \rangle \ldots \langle y_{\ell} \rangle \langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_{m} \rangle  \langle s_1 \rangle \ldots \langle s_n \rangle$ and $\beta_1:=(\sigma(y_1)/y_1)^{\lambda_1-1}$.
By application of Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem}~(iv), there is a difference ring isomorphism
 \[\begin{array}{cccc}
\tau: & (u_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda_1}) & \rightarrow & (\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \tilde{\sigma}_0) \\
      & u_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}
\end{array}
\] 
for every $f \in {\mathbb{E}}$, where the automorphism $\tilde{\sigma}_0$ on $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ is given by $$\tilde{\sigma}_0(\tilde{f}):=\sigma^{\lambda_1}(\tilde{f})|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$$ for every $\tilde{f} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. Moreover, $\tau^{-1}(\tilde{f})=u_0\tilde{f}$ for every $\tilde{f} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. 
On the other hand, by Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem} (vi), $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \tilde{\sigma}_0)$ is an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}, \sigma^{\lambda_1})$.

If $\ell=1$, then the proposition holds  by Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem} (v). 

Assume that $\ell>1$  and the conclusion holds for $\ell-1$. 
By Remark \ref{RE:constant-stable}, $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda_1})=C$ and $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda_1})$ is a constant-stable integral domain. Note that the number of generators in $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ is equal to $\ell-1$ and the product of the orders of $R$-monomials in $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ is equal to $\lambda/\lambda_1$,  denoted by $\mu$.
So by the induction hypothesis, 
\begin{itemize}
  \item  one can compute $v_0, v_1, \ldots, v_{\mu-1} \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ such that $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \tilde{\sigma}_0)$ is an idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{v_i~|~i\in [\mu-1]_0\}$;
  \item there is  $\{\beta_i\in C~|~2 \le i \le k\}$ such that $({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0)$, where for every $h\in {\mathbb{H}}$, $\sigma_0(h):=\tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu}(h)|_{\{y_i \rightarrow \beta_i | 2\le i \le \ell\}}$,  is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. 
Moreover, we obtain a difference ring isomorphism
\[\begin{array}{cccc}
\tilde{\delta}: & (v_0\tilde {\mathbb{E}},\tilde \sigma_0^\mu) & \rightarrow & ({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0) \\
      & v_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{\{y_i \rightarrow \beta_i\mid 2\le i\le \ell\}},
\end{array}
\] 
for every $f\in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$, and $\tilde{\delta}^{-1}(h)=v_0h$ for every $h\in {\mathbb{H}}$.
\end{itemize}

The proof will be completed by the following three claims.

\noindent{\bf Claim~1.} For every $f \in {\mathbb{E}}$ and $i \in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$, $\tilde{\sigma}_0^i(f|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1})=\sigma^{\lambda_1i}(f)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$.

{\em Proof of Claim~1.} Write $f$ as $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda_1-1}f_iy_1^i$, where $f_i \in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. Then
$$\tilde{\sigma}_0(f|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1})=\tilde{\sigma}_0\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda_1-1}f_i \beta_1^i\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda_1-1}\beta_1^i \sigma^{\lambda_1}(f_i)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$$
by $\beta_1\in C$, and
$$\sigma^{\lambda_1}(f)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda_1-1}(\sigma^{\lambda_1}(f_i)y_1^i)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda_1-1}\beta_1^i \sigma^{\lambda_1}(f_i)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$$
by $\sigma^{\lambda_1}(y_1)=y_1$. So $\tilde{\sigma}_0(f|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1})=\sigma^{\lambda_1}(f)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$. Hence, the claim is completed by induction on $i$.
 
In the rest of this proof, set $e_0:=u_0v_0$. 

\noindent{\bf Claim~2.} For every $h \in {\mathbb{H}}$, $\sigma_0(h)=\sigma^{\lambda}(h)|_{\{y_i \rightarrow \beta_i \mid i \in [\ell]\}}$. Furthermore, $\delta:=\tilde{\delta} \circ \tau|_{e_0{\mathbb{E}}}$ is a difference ring isomorphism from $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda})$ onto $({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0)$ by sending $e_0f$ to $f|_{\{y_i \rightarrow \beta_i\mid i\in [\ell]\}}$. Moreover, $\delta^{-1}(h)=e_0h$ for every $h\in {\mathbb{H}}$.

{\em Proof of Claim~2.} Since $h$ is free of $y_1$ and $\lambda_1\mu=\lambda$, we have 
$\tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu}(h)=\tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu}(h|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1})=\sigma^{\lambda}(h)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$ by Claim 1. It follows that $\sigma_0(h)=\sigma^{\lambda}(h)|_{\{y_i \rightarrow \beta_i \mid i \in [\ell]\}}$ by the definition of $\sigma_0$. 
Set $\tilde{\tau}:=\tau|_{e_0{\mathbb{E}}}$. Then $\tilde{\tau}$ is a ring isomorphism from $e_0{\mathbb{E}}$ onto $v_0\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ by mapping $e_0f$ to $(v_0 f)|_{y_1\rightarrow \beta_1}$ for every $f\in {\mathbb{E}}$. On the other hand, $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda})$ and $(v_0\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu})$ are two difference rings. Moreover,
$$\tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu}(\tilde{\tau}(e_0f))=\tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu}((v_0f)|_{y_1\rightarrow \beta_1})=\sigma^{\lambda}(v_0f)|_{y_1\rightarrow \beta_1}=\tau(u_0\sigma^{\lambda}(v_0f))=\tilde{\tau}(e_0\sigma^{\lambda}(f))$$
by Claim~1. So $\tilde{\tau}$ is a difference ring isomorphism from $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda})$ onto $(v_0\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \tilde{\sigma}_0^{\mu})$. Hence, 
$\delta:=\tilde{\delta} \circ \tilde{\tau}$ is a difference ring isomorphism from $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda})$ onto $({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0)$. Note that $v_0$ is free of $y_1$. So $\delta(e_0f)=\tilde{\delta}(v_0\cdot (f|_{y_1\rightarrow \beta_1}))=f|_{\{y_i \rightarrow \beta_i\mid i\in [\ell]\}}$. Furthermore, for every $h\in {\mathbb{H}}$, $\delta^{-1}(h)=\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(\tilde{\delta}^{-1}(h))=\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(v_0h)=e_0h$. 


\noindent{\bf Claim~3.} ${\mathbb{E}}= e_0{\mathbb{E}} \oplus\sigma( e_0){\mathbb{E}}\oplus \cdots \oplus\sigma^{\lambda-1} ( e_{0}){\mathbb{E}}.$

{\em Proof of Claim~3.} Recall that $\tau^{-1}$ is a difference ring isomorphism from $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}},\, \tilde{\sigma}_0)$ onto $(u_0{\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^{\lambda_1})$ given by $\tau^{-1}(f)=u_0f$ for every $f\in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$. Then, for every $j \in [\mu-1]_0$, 
 $$\tau^{-1}(\tilde{\sigma}_0^j (v_0))=\tau^{-1}(\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(v_0)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1})=u_0(\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(v_0)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1})=u_0\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(v_0)=\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(e_0).$$
  The first and third equalities are due to Claim 1 and Lemma~\ref{LM:anRmonomial+idem}~(ii), respectively. Furthermore, it follows from $u_0^2=u_0$ and $\sigma^{\lambda_1}(u_0)=u_0$ that $\tau^{-1}(\tilde{\sigma}_0^j (v_0)\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}})=\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(e_0)u_0{\mathbb{E}}=\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(u_0e_0){\mathbb{E}}=\sigma^{\lambda_1j}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}$. By Lemma~\ref{LM:idemp+equivdef}, we have $\tilde {\mathbb{E}}=v_0\tilde {\mathbb{E}}\oplus \tilde\sigma_0(v_0)\tilde {\mathbb{E}}\oplus\cdots \oplus  \tilde\sigma_0^{\mu-1}(v_0)\tilde {\mathbb{E}}$. Applying $\tau^{-1}$ to the above direct sum yields 

 $$u_0{\mathbb{E}}=e_0{\mathbb{E}} \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \oplus \cdots \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1(\mu-1)}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}.$$
Recall that $\sigma(u_i)=u_{i+1  \! \bmod \! \lambda_1}$. Then applying $\sigma, \sigma^2, \ldots, \sigma^{\lambda_1-1}$ to the above equation  successively leads to
\[
  \left\{
\begin{aligned}
u_0{\mathbb{E}}& =e_0{\mathbb{E}} \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \oplus \cdots \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1(\mu-1)}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}\\
u_1{\mathbb{E}}  &= \sigma(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1+1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}\oplus \cdots \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1(\mu-1)+1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \\
\vdots \\
  u_i{\mathbb{E}}   &= \sigma^i(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1+i}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}\oplus \cdots \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1(\mu-1)+i}(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \\
   \vdots \\
   u_{\lambda_1-1}{\mathbb{E}}  &= \sigma^{\lambda_1-1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}} \oplus \sigma^{2\lambda_1-1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}\oplus \cdots \oplus \sigma^{\lambda_1\mu-1}(e_0){\mathbb{E}}.
\end{aligned}
\right.
\]
Recall that $\lambda=\lambda_1\mu$. Arranging the right-hand side of the above system in an $S$-shaped order, we obtain
 $${\mathbb{E}}=u_0{\mathbb{E}} \oplus u_1{\mathbb{E}}\oplus \cdots \oplus u_{\lambda_1-1}{\mathbb{E}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\lambda-1} \sigma^i(e_0){\mathbb{E}},$$
 which proves the third claim.\\
Finally, we are ready to show that the conclusion holds for the case $\ell$. The second assertion follows from Claim 2 and the induction hypothesis. For every $i \in [\lambda-1]$, let $e_i:=\sigma^{i}(e_0)$. Then ${\mathbb{E}}$ is a weak idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i \in [\lambda-1]_0\}$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:idemp+equivdef} and Claim 3. Moreover, we note that ${\mathbb{H}}$ is an integral domain, so is $e_0{\mathbb{E}}$ by the second assertion. Hence, the first assertion holds.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- alg-8: body_md -->
\label{ALG:IdemDecomp}
 {\tt IdempotentDecompositionsFor$R\Pi\Sigma^*$-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$
 
  \noindent
 {\tt Input:} 
An $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ over a computable, constant-stable, integral difference ring $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ with constant field $C$, of
the form
${\mathbb{E}} =
{\mathbb{A}}\langle y_1\rangle\ldots\langle y_\ell\rangle
\langle p_1\rangle\ldots\langle p_m\rangle
\langle s_1\rangle\ldots\langle s_n\rangle,
$
where $\ell>0$, and the $y_i$, $p_i$, and $s_i$ are $R$-, $\Pi$-, and $\Sigma^*$-monomials, respectively.

 
 \noindent
 {\tt Output:} $\{(e_0, \lambda), ({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0), \delta\}$ such that
 ${\mathbb{E}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\lambda-1} \sigma^i(e_0){\mathbb{E}}$ with the idempotent $e_0 \in {\mathbb{E}}$ and $\lambda\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$; $({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$ with constant field $C$  and $\delta$ is a difference ring isomorphism from $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda})$ onto  $({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)$.
 
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[(1)] Set $a_1:=\frac{\sigma(y_1)}{y_1}$, $\lambda_1:=\operatorname{ord}(y_1)$ and $u_0:=\prod_{j=0}^{\lambda_1-2}\frac{y_1-a_1^j}{a_1^{\lambda_1-1}-a_1^j}$.
	
	\item[(2)] Set $\beta_1:=a_1^{\lambda_1-1}$ and $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}:=C(x)\langle y_2 \rangle \ldots \langle y_{\ell} \rangle \langle p_1 \rangle \ldots \langle p_{m} \rangle  \langle s_1 \rangle \ldots \langle s_{n} \rangle$ together with $\tilde{\sigma}_0(f):=\sigma^{\lambda_1}(f)|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}$ for  $f\in \widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$, and  
\[\begin{array}{cccc}
		\tau: & (u_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda_1}) & \rightarrow & (\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \,\tilde{\sigma}_0) \\
		& u_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{y_1 \rightarrow \beta_1}.
	\end{array}
	\] 
	\item [(3)] $(^*${\sl Base case}$^*)$ If $\ell=1$ then return $\{(u_0, \lambda_1), (\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \tilde{\sigma}_0), \tau\}$. 	
	\item [(4)] $(^*${\sl Recursion}$^*)$ Note that the number of $R$-monomials in $\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}$ is $\ell-1$. 
	So using the algorithm, the output for $(\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}, \tilde{\sigma}_0)$ is $\{(v_0, \mu), ({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0), \tilde{\delta}\}$.
	\item [(5)] Return $\{(u_0v_0, \lambda_1\mu), ({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0),\tilde{\delta} \circ \tau|_{u_0v_0{\mathbb{E}}}\}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-8 -->
<!-- example-9: body_md -->
\label{EX:IdemDecomp}
Let $C={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1})$ and $(C(x),\,\sigma)$ be the rational difference field with $\sigma(x)=x+1$. Consider the $R$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ over $(C(x),\,\sigma)$ with
 ${\mathbb{E}}=C(x)\langle y_1 \rangle \langle y_2 \rangle$ of $C(x)$ and  $\sigma(y_1)=-y_1$ and $\sigma(y_2)=(\frac{1-y_1}{2}+\frac{1+y_1}{2} \sqrt{-1})y_2$ ; compare Example~\ref{EX:CheckRmonomials}. We will compute an idempotent representation of $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ by using Algorithm~\ref{ALG:IdemDecomp}. 

In step 1, $a_1=-1$, $\lambda_1=2$, $u_0=\frac{1-y_1}{2}$. Further, we get the difference ring isomorphism 
\[\begin{array}{cccc}
\tau: & (u_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^{\lambda_1}) & \rightarrow & (C(x)\langle y_2 \rangle, \tilde{\sigma}_0) \\
      & u_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{y_1 \rightarrow -1}
\end{array}
\] 
 with $\tilde{\sigma}_0(f)=\sigma^2(f)|_{y_1\rightarrow -1}$ for every $f \in C(x)\langle y_2 \rangle$ by Example~\ref{EX:CheckRmonomials} again. In particular, $\tilde{\sigma}_0(y_2)= \sqrt{-1}y_2$. Next, we go directly to step 4. By recursion, $\mu=4$,
 $$v_0=\frac{y_2^3-\sqrt{-1}y_2^2-y_2+\sqrt{-1}}{4\sqrt{-1}},$$
 and there is a difference ring isomorphism
 \[\begin{array}{cccc}
\tilde{\delta}: & (v_0C(x)\langle y_2 \rangle, \sigma^8) & \rightarrow & (C(x), \sigma^8) \\
      & v_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{y_2 \rightarrow -\sqrt{-1}}.
\end{array}
\] 
Therefore, $\bigoplus_{i=0}^{7}\sigma^i(u_0v_0){\mathbb{E}}$ is an idempotent representation of $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$. Furthermore, $(u_0v_0{\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ and $(C(x), \sigma^8)$ are isomorphic by sending $u_0v_0f$ to $f|_{\{y_1 \rightarrow -1, y_2 \rightarrow -\sqrt{-1}\}}$ for every $f\in {\mathbb{E}}$.
<!-- end example-9 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{LM:projecttothefirstcompotent}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be an idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$ and $\lambda>1$.  Then, for every $f\in {\mathbb{A}}$, 
  $f=\Delta(v)+e_0\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{-i}(f)$
for $v=\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
First we have $f=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}e_if$ by \eqref{EQ:idempotentrepresentation}. For $i\in [\lambda-1]$, set $u_i$ to be $\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)$. It follows from telescoping that $e_if=\Delta(u_i)+e_0\sigma^{-i}(f)$. So $f=\Delta(\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}u_i)+e_0\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{-i}(f).$
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{LM:lambdatozero}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be a difference ring, $\lambda > 1$, and $f\in {\mathbb{A}}$. If $f=\Delta^{(\lambda)}(g)$ for some $g \in {\mathbb{A}}$, then there is $\tilde{g}=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(g)$ such that $f=\Delta(\tilde{g})$. In particular, $ \Delta^{(\lambda)}({\mathbb{A}}) \subset \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
This is an immediate consequence of telescoping cancellation.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{LM:convert}
Let $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma)$ be an idempotent difference ring with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$ and $\lambda>1$. Assume that the set $C$ of constants in ${\mathbb{A}}$ is a field. Define the map
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
\psi:& {\mathbb{A}} & \rightarrow & e_0{\mathbb{A}} \\
      & f & \mapsto     & e_0\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{-i}(f)
\end{array}
\]
and let $\phi$ be a complete reduction for $(e_0{\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta^{(\lambda)})$ that is $e_0C$-linear. 
Then
\begin{itemize}
\item [(i)]  $\psi$ is $C$-linear with $\psi(e_jf)=e_0\sigma^{-j}(f)$ for every $f \in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $j\in[\lambda-1]_0$.
 \item [(ii)] $\phi \circ \psi$ is a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$ as a $C$-linear map.
 \item [(iii)] Let $f\in {\mathbb{A}}$. Then $(\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(g), r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair with respect to $\phi \circ \psi$ if $(g, r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $\psi(f)$ with respect to $\phi$.
 \item[(iv)] Let $f\in{\mathbb{A}}$. Then $f\in \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$ if and only if $\psi(f) \in \Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$. In particular, if there is  $\tilde{g} \in e_0{\mathbb{A}}$ such that $\psi(f)=\Delta^{(\lambda)}(\tilde{g})$, then $f=\Delta(g)$ with $g=\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(\tilde{g})$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
(i) Let $f\in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $i,j\in [\lambda-1]$. Then $\psi$ is $C$-linear by $\sigma^{-i}(cf)=c\sigma^{-i}(f)$ for every $c\in C$. Consider $e_0 \sigma^{-i}(e_j)$. It is equal to $e_0$ if $i=j$, and 0 otherwise. So $\psi(e_jf)=e_0\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{-i}(e_jf)=e_0\sigma^{-j}(e_jf)=e_0\sigma^{-j}(f)$.

(ii) Since $C$ is a field and $e_0$ is the multiplicative identity of $e_0C$, $e_0C$ is a subfield of $e_0{\mathbb{A}}$. Moreover, $e_0C \subset \operatorname{const}(e_0{\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. So $\phi$ is well-defined. 
Let $f\in {\mathbb{A}}$ and $c\in C$. 
Then $$\phi(\psi(cf))=\phi(c\psi(f))=\phi(ce_0\psi(f))=ce_0(\phi(\psi(f)))=c\phi(\psi(f)).$$
The first and third equalities follow from the fact that $\psi$ and $\phi$ are $C$-linear and $e_0C$-linear, respectively. 
The second and fourth equalities hold because $\operatorname{im}(\psi) \subset e_0{\mathbb{A}}$ and $r=e_0r$ for all $r \in e_0{\mathbb{A}}$,  as noted in Remark \ref{RE:idemp+unit}~(i).
Hence, $\phi \circ \psi$ is $C$-linear. Next, we are going to show that $\phi \circ \psi$ is a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$. 
Since $\phi(\psi(f)) \in e_0{\mathbb{A}}$, we have $\psi(\phi(\psi(f)))=\phi(\psi(f))$ by (i), which, together with $\phi^2=\phi$, implies that $\phi \circ \psi \circ \phi \circ \psi(f)=\phi \circ \psi(f).$ So $\phi \circ \psi$ is an idempotent map. 

By Remark \ref{RE:cr}, it remains to show $\Delta({\mathbb{A}})=\ker(\phi \circ \psi)$. Assume that $f\in \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$. Then there is $g\in {\mathbb{A}}$ such that $f=\Delta(g)$. According to \eqref{EQ:idempotentrepresentation}, we expand $\Delta(g)$ as $\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}e_i\Delta(g)$. Then, by (i) again,
$\psi(e_i\Delta(g))=e_0\sigma^{-i}(\sigma(g)-g)=e_0(\sigma^{1-i}(g)-\sigma^{-i}(g)).$ This yields
$$\psi(f)=e_0\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}(\sigma^{1-i}(g)-\sigma^{-i}(g))=e_0(\sigma(g)-\sigma^{1-\lambda}(g))=\sigma^{\lambda}(\tilde{g})-\tilde{g} $$
for $\tilde{g}=e_0\sigma^{1-\lambda}(g)$. So $\psi(f)\in \Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$. Hence, $f \in \ker(\phi \circ \psi)$ by $\ker(\phi)=\Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$. Conversely, let $f \in \ker(\phi \circ \psi)$. Then $\psi(f) \in \Delta^{(\lambda)}({\mathbb{A}})$ by $e_0{\mathbb{A}} \subset {\mathbb{A}}$ and $\ker(\phi)=\Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$ again.  Lemma~\ref{LM:lambdatozero} ensures that $\psi(f) \in \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$. Consequently, $f \in \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:projecttothefirstcompotent}.

(iii) Set $v$ to be $\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)$.  By Lemma~\ref{LM:projecttothefirstcompotent}, $f=\Delta(v)+\psi(f)$.  Let $(g, r)$ be a $\Sigma$-pair of $\psi(f)$ with respect to $\phi$.
Then $f=\Delta(v)+\Delta^{(\lambda)}(g)+r=\Delta(v+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(g))+r$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:lambdatozero}. Note that $r=\phi \circ \psi(f) \in \operatorname{im}(\phi \circ \psi)$. So $(v+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(g), r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $f$ with respect to  $\phi \circ \psi$.

(iv) By (ii), $\phi \circ \psi$ is a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \Delta)$. So $\Delta({\mathbb{A}})=\ker(\phi \circ \psi)$, which implies that $f\in \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$ if and only if $f\in \ker(\phi \circ \psi)$, i.e., $\psi(f) \in \ker(\phi)$. Moreover, $\ker(\phi)=\Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$ by the definition of $\phi$.
It follows that  $\psi(f)\in \ker(\phi)$ if and only if $\psi(f)\in \Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$. Thus, $f\in \Delta({\mathbb{A}})$ if and only if $\psi(f) \in \Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{A}})$. The remaining conclusion follows directly from (iii).
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
\label{THM:CR+RPiSigma}
	Let $({\mathbb{A}},\,\sigma)$ be a computable difference ring which is constant-stable and an integral domain, and  let $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ be an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}}, \, \sigma)$. Assume that the set of constants $C$ forms a field. Then the following assertions hold.
	\begin{itemize}
    \item [(i)] If one can solve the telescoping problem for every $ i\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and every $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{i})$,
    then it can also be  solved for $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$.
    \item [(ii)] If  
    ${\mathbb{A}}$ has an effective $C$-basis, and there exists an algorithm which, for every $i\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$ and $f \in {\mathbb{A}}^*$, constructs a complete reduction $\phi_f^{(i)}$ for $({\mathbb{A}},\, \Delta^{(i)}_{f})$. Then there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\omega$ for $({\mathbb{E}}, \, \Delta)$. 
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
If ${\mathbb{E}}$ is free of $R$-monomials, then (i) is trivial and (ii) follows by Theorem~\ref{THM:SimpleRPiSigmaExt}. 
Otherwise,  
let ${\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_1 \rangle \langle t_2 \rangle \ldots \langle t_k \rangle$. Define 
$${\mathbb{I}}:=\{1\le i \le k~|~t_i~\text{is an $R$-monomial}\}\neq\emptyset$$ 
and $\lambda:=\prod_{i\in {\mathbb{I}}} \operatorname{ord}(t_i)$. Moreover, set ${\mathbb{H}}:={\mathbb{A}}\langle t_i\mid i\in [k]\setminus {\mathbb{I}}\rangle$.
By Proposition~\ref{PROP:RPiSigmaIsIdempotent}~(i) (for details see Algorithm~\ref{ALG:IdemDecomp}), one can compute $e_0,\ldots,e_{\lambda-1} \in {\mathbb{E}}$ such that $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is an idempotent difference ring  with respect to $\{e_i~|~i\in[\lambda-1]_0\}$. And
there is $\{\beta_i\in C~|~i\in {\mathbb{I}} \}$ such that $({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)$ with $\sigma_0(h)=\sigma^{\lambda}(h)|_{\{t_i \rightarrow \beta_i|i \in {\mathbb{I}}\}}$ for every $h \in {\mathbb{H}}$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{A}},\, \sigma^{\lambda})$. Moreover, we get a difference ring isomorphism
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
	\delta: & (e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^\lambda) & \rightarrow & ({\mathbb{H}}, \sigma_0) \\
	& e_0f& \mapsto     & f|_{\{t_i \rightarrow \beta_i\mid i\in{\mathbb{I}}\}}
\end{array}
\]
for every $f \in {\mathbb{E}}$ and $\delta^{-1}(h)=e_0h$ for every $h \in {\mathbb{H}}$. Note that $\operatorname{const}({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)=C$. 
Note further that a complete reduction for $(e_0{\mathbb{A}}, \,\Delta^{(\lambda)})$ that is $e_0C$-linear always exists, as assumed in Lemma~\ref{LM:convert};
 we will use the existence in statement (i), but rely on an explicit construction in statement (ii).

Let $\psi$ be a $C$-linear function from ${\mathbb{E}}$ to $e_0{\mathbb{E}}$ by sending $f$ to $e_0\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{-i}(f)$. And set $\rho:=\sigma_0-\bf{1}$. 

(i) Let $f\in{\mathbb{E}}$. Then $f\in \Delta({\mathbb{E}})$ if and only if $\psi(f) \in \Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{E}})$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:convert} (iv). Moreover, $\psi(f) \in \Delta^{(\lambda)}(e_0{\mathbb{E}})$ if and only $\delta(\psi(f)) \in \rho({\mathbb{H}})$. By the assumption, the latter statement can be decided.  
If there is $g' \in {\mathbb{H}}$ such that $\rho(g')=\delta(\psi(f))$, then $\delta^{-1} \circ \sigma^{\lambda}=\sigma_0 \circ \delta$ implies that $\psi(f)=\Delta^{(\lambda)}(\delta^{-1}(g'))$. Thus, $f=\Delta(g)$ with $g=\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{i}(\delta^{-1}(g'))$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:convert} (iv) again.

(ii) Under the assumption of statement (ii), there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\Phi$ as a $C$-linear map for $({\mathbb{H}},\, \rho)$ by Theorem~\ref{THM:SimpleRPiSigmaExt}. 
By Lemma~\ref{LM:diffiso+CR}, $\phi:=\delta^{-1} \circ \Phi \circ \delta$ is a complete reduction for $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\, \Delta^{(\lambda)})$.
Moreover, $\omega:=\phi\circ \psi$ is a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{E}},\,\Delta)$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:convert}~(ii). It remains to describe how to compute $\Sigma$-pairs with respect to $\omega$. Let $f\in{\mathbb{E}}$ and denote $\psi(f)$ by $f'$. Then by assumption, we can compute a $\Sigma$-pair $(g',r')$ of $\delta(f')$ with respect to $\Phi$. 
 Then $(g, r):=(\delta^{-1}(g'),\delta^{-1}(r'))$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $f'$ with respect to $\phi$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:diffiso+CR}. Finally, $(\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}e_i\sigma^{i}(g), r)$ is a $\Sigma$-pair of $f$ with respect to $\omega$ by Lemma~\ref{LM:convert}~(iii).
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- cor-2: body_md -->
\label{Cor:PiSiOverRPiSiTelescoper}
	Let $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ be an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-field whose constant field $C$ is orbit-computable. Then, for every $g\in{\mathbb{E}}$, one can decide algorithmically if there is $q\in{\mathbb{E}}$ with $\Delta(q)=g$.
<!-- end cor-2 -->
<!-- cor-3: body_md -->
\label{COR:CR+RPiSigma+Rational}
Let ${\mathbb{F}}=C(x)$ be the rational difference field with $\sigma(x)=x+1$, where $C$ is factorizable, and let $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ be an $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $({\mathbb{F}},\, \sigma)$. Then there is an algorithm for constructing a complete reduction $\omega$ for $({\mathbb{E}},\, \Delta)$.
<!-- end cor-3 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Note that $({\mathbb{F}},\, \sigma)$ is constant-stable by \cite[Proposition~32]{Schn2020}. Then the result follows from  Proposition~\ref{PROP:Generalrational+highorder} and Theorem~\ref{THM:CR+RPiSigma}~(ii).
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- alg-9: body_md -->
\label{ALG:SigmaPairinGeneralRPiSigma}
{\tt CRFor$R\Pi\Sigma^*$-Towers}$({\mathbb{E}}, g)$

	\noindent
	{\tt Input:} an  $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ over $(C(x),\,\sigma)$ with $\sigma(x)=x+1$, where $C$ is factorizable; $g\in{\mathbb{E}}$.
	
	\noindent
	{\tt Output:} a $\Sigma$-pair of $g$ with respect to $\omega$ as given in Corollary \ref{COR:CR+RPiSigma+Rational}.
\begin{enumerate}

\item[(1)] If $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower, use Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPiSigma-Towers} to derive the desired result. Otherwise, we proceed as follows.

\item[(2)] Applying Algorithm~\ref{ALG:IdemDecomp} to $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ yields $\{(e_0, \lambda), ({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0),\delta\}$.
Here $e_0\in{\mathbb{E}}$ is an idempotent and ${\mathbb{E}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^i(e_0){\mathbb{E}}$. Moreover, $({\mathbb{H}},\,\sigma_0)$ is a $\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower over $(C(x),\sigma^\lambda)$, and $\delta$ is a difference ring isomorphism from $(e_0{\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma^\lambda)$ onto $({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)$.
    
\item[(3)] Set $\psi$ to be a $C$-linear function from ${\mathbb{E}}$ to $e_0{\mathbb{E}}$ by sending $f$ to $e_0\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}\sigma^{-i}(f)$. 
Using Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPiSigma-Towers}, we get a $\Sigma$-pair $(u,v)$ of $\delta(\psi(f))$ with respect to a complete reduction $\Phi$ for $({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0-\bf{1})$.
 
 \item [(4)] 
Return $$(\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}\sum_{j=1}^i e_{i-j} \sigma^{-j}(f)+\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}e_i\sigma^{i}(u), e_0v).$$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-9 -->
<!-- example-10: body_md -->
\label{EX:newidentity}
Simplify    
	\[\sum_{j=0}^n \frac{((-1)^j(2-j)+j)j!}{2^j \lfloor \frac j2\rfloor!}.\]
 We start with the rational difference field $({\mathbb{Q}}(x), \, \sigma)$ with $\sigma(x)=x+1$ and construct the $R\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma)$ over $({\mathbb{Q}}(x),\sigma)$ with ${\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{Q}}(x)\langle y\rangle \langle p_1\rangle \langle p_2\rangle \langle p_3\rangle$ and
   \[\sigma(y)=-y, \, \sigma(p_1)=2p_1, \, \sigma(p_2)=(x+1)p_2 \,\, \text{and} \,\, \sigma(p_3)= ap_3,\]
   where $a=\frac {x+3-y(x-1)}{4}$. By a direct computation, $\left(\frac{1-y}{x+1}+\frac{1+y}{2}\right)a=1$. So $a\in{\mathbb{Q}}(x)\langle y\rangle \langle p_1\rangle \langle p_2\rangle^*$. Since $a$ is not a product of $y, p_1$ and $p_2$, ${\mathbb{E}}$ is not simple.  
   On the other hand, $y, p_1$ and $p_2$ model $(-1)^n$, $2^j$ and $j!$, respectively. Note that $\lfloor \frac j2\rfloor!$ can be expressed as $\prod_{k=1}^{j}\frac{1}{4}(k+2+(-1)^k(k-2))$. So $p_3$ models $\lfloor \frac j2\rfloor!$. It follows that the given summand can be represented by 
   $$f=\frac{(y (2 - x) + x) p_2}{p_1 p_3} \in {\mathbb{E}}.$$ 
   In this setting, we use Algorithm~\ref{ALG:SigmaPairinGeneralRPiSigma} to compute a $\Sigma$-pair of $f$ with respect to $\omega$ given in Corollary~\ref{COR:CR+RPiSigma+Rational}. 
   In step~2, we get the idempotent representation $e_0{\mathbb{E}} \oplus \sigma(e_0){\mathbb{E}}$ of $({\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma)$ with $e_0=\frac{1-y}2$. And we obtain the $\Pi$-tower $({\mathbb{H}},\sigma_0)$ over $({\mathbb{Q}}(x),\sigma^2)$ with ${\mathbb{H}}={\mathbb{Q}}(x) \langle p_1\rangle \langle p_2\rangle \langle p_3\rangle$ and
       \[\sigma_0(p_1)=4p_1, \, \sigma_0(p_2)=(x+1)(x+2)p_2, \, \text{and} \, \sigma_0(p_3)= \frac{1+x}2 p_3.\]
    Moreover, there is a difference ring isomorphism $\delta\colon (e_0 {\mathbb{E}},\, \sigma^2) \to ({\mathbb{H}},\, \sigma_0)$ by sending $g$ to $g|_{y\to -1}$ for every $g\in e_0{\mathbb{E}}$. In step 3,  $\delta(\psi(f))=\frac{1-y}2(f+\sigma^{-1}(f))|_{y\to -1}=2 x^{-1}(2 - x + x^2)p_1^{-1}p_2p_3^{-1}$. Applying Algorithm~\ref{ALG:CRForSimpleRPiSigma-Towers} to
     $({\mathbb{H}},\sigma_0)$ and $\delta(\psi(f))$, we obtain a $\Sigma$-pair $(u, 0)$ of $\delta(\psi(f))$, where $u=4  (x-1)p_2x^{-1}p_1^{-1} p_3^{-1}$. Finally, we conclude that
     $$\left((2+x-2y+xy)p_2p_1^{-1}p_3^{-1}, 0\right)$$
     is a $\Sigma$-pair of $f$ with respect to $\omega$. Rephrasing this result to our summation objects yields the identity
      \[\sum_{j=0}^n \frac{((-1)^j(2-j)+j)j!}{2^j \lfloor \frac j2\rfloor!}=\frac{2(n+1)!}{2^n \lfloor \frac n2\rfloor!}.\]
<!-- end example-10 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
\label{PROP:telescoper}
With the notation introduced above, let $\omega$ be a complete reduction for $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma_k-\bf{1})$. 
For $f\in {\mathbb{E}}$ and $m\in {\mathbb{N}}$, $f$ has a telescoper of order  $m$  if and only if there exist $c_0,\ldots, c_m\in C$ with $c_m \neq 0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{EQ:telescopingequation}
c_0\omega(f)+c_1 \omega(\sigma_n(f))+\cdots+c_m\omega(\sigma_n^m(f))=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Let $L=\sum_{i=0}^{m} c_i \sigma_n^i$ for $c_0, \ldots, c_m$ with $c_m \neq 0$ and $(g,r)$ be a $\Sigma$-pair of $L(f)$ with respect to $\omega$. Then $\omega(L(f))=\sum_{i=0}^{m} c_i \omega(\sigma_n^i(f))$ because $\omega$ is ${\mathbb{K}}(n)$-linear. Assume that \eqref{EQ:telescopingequation} holds, i.e., $r=0$. Then~\eqref{EQ:paratele} holds and 
$L$ is a telescoper for $f$ with order $m$. Conversely, assume that $L$ is a telescoper for $f$ of order $m$.  Since $\omega$ is a complete reduction, then $r=\omega(L(f))=0$. So \eqref{EQ:telescopingequation} holds.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- alg-10: body_md -->
{\tt CreativeTelescopingViaCR}$(f, {\mathbb{E}})$ \label{ALG:ParametricTelescoping}

 \noindent
{\tt Input:} An R$\Pi\Sigma^*$-tower $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma_k)$ over $({\mathbb{K}}(n, k),\, \{\sigma_n, \sigma_k\})$, where ${\mathbb{K}}$ is factorizable. Furthermore, an element $f\in {\mathbb{E}}$ where we assume that $f$ has a telescoper\footnote{This assertion is intended to guarantee that the loop below terminates. However, from a practical standpoint, it is often relaxed in the hope of finding a solution.}.

 \noindent
{\tt Output:} A minimal telescoper $\sum_{i=0}^{m}c_i\sigma_n^i$ for $f$ and $g\in {\mathbb{E}}$ such that \eqref{EQ:paratele} holds.

\smallskip \noindent
\begin{enumerate}
	\item[(1)]  Use Algorithm~\ref{ALG:SigmaPairinGeneralRPiSigma} to compute a $\Sigma$-pair $(g_0, r_0)$ of $f$ with respect to a complete reduction $\omega$ for $({\mathbb{E}},\,\sigma_k-\mathbf{1})$
\item[(2)] If $r_0=0$, then return $(\mathbf{1}, g_0)$. Otherwise, set $g:=g_0$, $r:=\ell_0r_0$, where $\ell_0$ is a new constant indeterminate.
\item [(3)] For $m=1,2,3, \ldots$ do
\begin{itemize}
  \item [(3.1)] Use Algorithm~\ref{ALG:SigmaPairinGeneralRPiSigma} to compute  a $\Sigma$-pair $(g_m, r_m)$ of $\sigma_n(r_{m-1})$ with respect to $\omega$, and update $g, r$ to $\sigma_n(g)+\ell_mg_m$ and $r+\ell_mr_m$, where $\ell_m$ is a new constant indeterminate. 
 
  \item [(3.2)] Find $c_0,\ldots, c_{m} \in {\mathbb{K}}(n)$ such that $r=0$ by solving a linear system in $\ell_0, \ldots, \ell_m$ over ${\mathbb{K}}(n)$ (note that the coefficients of $r$ with respect to $\ell_i$ can be represented explicitly in a ${\mathbb{K}}(n)$-basis of ${\mathbb{E}}$; see the construction in the proof of Theorem \ref{THM:SimpleRPiSigmaExt}).\\ 
  If there is a nontrivial solution, return  $(\sum_{i=0}^{m}c_i\sigma_n^i,$  $g|_{\{ \ell_i \rightarrow c_j \mid i\in[m]_0\}})$
  \end{itemize}
 \end{enumerate}
<!-- end alg-10 -->
